Katalog Berita (Halaman 44/58)

#2997. Forget Anthropic: In Europe, the IPO hype is about a blue lagoon

While investors are fixated on Anthropic's blockbuster stock market debut, a very different IPO is making waves in Europe: Iceland's Blue Lagoon, the operator of one of the world's most famous geothermal spas.

Blue Lagoon Corp. raised roughly 14.3 billion Icelandic kronur ($110 million) through the offering, valuing the Icelandic geothermal spa operator at about $787 million, according to the company's investor presentation.

The Blue Lagoon traces its origins to 1992, when Blue Lagoon Ltd. was founded to study the water in the Blue Lagoon, an open-air pool of warm, milky-blue water set in a black lava field in southwestern Iceland. It grew into a business spanning a spa, hotels and skincare made with ingredients from the lagoon.

Shares will trade under the ticker "BLUE" on one of Europe's smallest stock exchanges, Nasdaq Iceland Main Market, part of the Nasdaq Nordic group, which also operates exchanges in Sweden, Denmark and Finland.

"Wellness is the new luxury, and we know that the wellness economy is growing faster than the economy as a whole, so we have tailwind from that," CEO Sigríður Margrét Oddsdóttir told CNBC on Thursday.

"Now is the right time for us to list," she added. "We are a company that thinks long-term, we have a clear vision for the next decade, and being publicly listed gives investors the opportunity to participate in our journey."

The company's operations are centered on Iceland's Reykjanes Peninsula, where, as well as the Blue Lagoon geothermal spa, it runs the Silica Hotel, the Retreat luxury hotel and spa, and the Michelin-starred Moss restaurant.

It also operates the Kerlingarfjöll Highland Base and Hrauneyjar properties in Iceland's highlands, and sells Blue Lagoon-branded skincare products internationally.

The 34-year-old company has averaged 11% annual revenue growth over the past decade, and next year is expected to be a record, with revenue growing by more than half, Oddsdóttir said.

The company plans to use the raised capital to fund expanding its existing properties, acquiring businesses, and developing new destinations.

In total, nearly 4,000 subscriptions were received in the initial public offering, worth about 15.3 billion kronur, about 7% more than the total size of the offering, the company said, citing strong demand from smaller investors, including retail investors.

Iceland's relatively small stock market had just 27 companies on its main exchange at the end of 2025, according to Nasdaq. The market is dominated by banks, seafood companies and other domestically focused firms.

The global wellness industry reached $7.5 trillion in 2025, up 8.9% from the previous year, according to the Global Wellness Institute. Spending on thermal and mineral springs grew 10.4% over the same period, with the institute forecasting annual growth of nearly 10% for the sector through 2030.

Tourism accounted for nearly 9% of Iceland's GDP in 2025, and is growing, according to Statistics Iceland. The country welcomed around 2.3 million international visitors that year, with the U.S. as its largest source market.

#2996. Pick Six: New York Jets at Los Angeles Chargers

The New York Jets are, embarrassingly, a three-point underdog on Sunday against a team who didn't earn their first win until less than a week ago. The Los Angeles Chargers will be looking to build on their 23-20 win over the Ravens, and the Jets are making the long trip out west to take them on.

Here are six storylines to watch as New York seeks to inject some positivity into their season:

Last week, we discussed the Jets' varying options for their slot cornerback position and they eventually went with one of our suggestions, which was to primarily use Minkah Fitzpatrick there in nickel packages, bringing Malachi Moore off the bench to fill in at safety.

The Jets potentially face a similar dilemma in the slot this week, albeit on offense. Both Omar Cooper and Isaiah Williams have been unable to practice, so if neither can go on Sunday, the Jets will have to make a change.

One option could be to go with the guys currently on the roster. You could reactivate Jelani Woods and Tim Patrick and either run more multiple tight end packages, or use players like Garrett Wilson there. Another option could be to bring up Sterling Shepard or Jamaal Pritchett from the practice squad.

The job share at running back remains a work in progress

The Jets are still trying to figure out how to get the best out of Braelon Allen and Breece Hall now that both are healthy at the same time. Allen's performances while Hall was out proved that he can benefit from a bigger workload. However, Hall brings different things to the table in the passing game, so the Jets will want him on the field more often than not.

Last week was interesting because they employed Hall more in the passing game in the first half with Allen getting more carries. At the half, Allen had six carries and Hall had just two. However, after halftime, Allen carried the ball just twice and Hall had nine carries. It's possible the Jets' thinking was that Hall might produce better after halftime if he was fresh and the defense was tired. In the end, the pair had similar rushing numbers, but Hall produced more as a receiver.

You can probably expect the Jets to keep working on this with one option being flipping last week's gameplan on its head and using Hall early, then keeping Allen fresh to be the hammer in the second half. The Chargers' run defense did a great job against the Ravens, holding them to 16 yards on 15 carries, so it might not be easy to get a running game established.

The Chargers' leading tackler is linebacker Daiyan Henley, but he's been managing a knee issue all season and has been missing practice this week.

Henley, who is also second on the team in defensive snaps played, has been exploited in coverage at times this year, but is still a key contributor to the Chargers' run defense and tends to be the guy who wears the defensive headset.

If Henley can't go, the next man up would be Marlowe Wax, who has played under 100 defensive snaps in his career.

Chargers quarterback Justin Herbert has been struggling in the passing game. A large part of that is that the Chargers have struggled to protect him. Herbert has been sacked 33 times so far this season; more than every NFL quarterback apart from Deshaun Watson. Only two of their starting offensive linemen have started all nine games, which hasn't helped matters.

Last week, the Jets increased Will McDonald's workload as they had promised to, but it meant that Kingsley Enagbare only got to rush the passer a season-low 15 times. With Joseph Ossai now also back in the rotation, sustaining McDonald's level of opportunities might prove tricky. He did respond with a sack last week, though.

The Jets's run defense continues to struggle with two of their starting interior defensive linemen on injured reserve, although one – David Onyemata – was activated for practice this week, so is eligible to return.

New York gave up over 200 yards to the Bills, including a back-breaking 74-yard touchdown in the fourth quarter. The damage could have been even worse had it not been for the efforts of Harrison Phillips, though, as he stopped 10 runs for a gain of four or less.

Los Angeles has the NFL's worst scoring offense but they actually boast a top-10 running game and are coming off a game against the Ravens where Omarion Hampton rushed for over 100 yards for the first time all year. In addition, Herbert gave them a boost with a season-high 51 rushing yards, including his first touchdown.

When the Jets won games last year, a feature of these performances was good production in the return game but that hasn't been happening in 2026. If Williams is out this week, maybe that wouldn't be the worst thing, because he's been much less productive than last season.

In last week's game, the Jets used Isaiah Davis on kickoffs and he did quite well, having always put up solid numbers in the past. If Williams is out, then they may elevate someone like Pritchett to return punts, although the depth chart on the team's official website lists Davis as the backup punt returner too.

The bad news is that the Chargers' coverage numbers are pretty solid, led by ex-Jets linebacker Del'Shawn Phillips who was the NFL's leader in special teams tackles last season. On kickoffs, Cameron Dicker has allowed the third lowest return average in the NFL and their punter JK Scott is the number one graded punter according to Pro Football Focus rankings.

Let us know how you see things going in Sunday's game in the comments section

#2994. Game Preview: Nets look to snap losing streak against Magic in NBA Cup

The team hosted Julius Erving and some of the ABA championship teams. On live television, Dr. J innocently said he "fully anticipated the Nets to be so fired up having us here" as the team lost by 30. Veteran leader Julius Randle said it was one of the worst games he's been part of in his 13-year career. Head coach Jordi Fernandez continues to say effort is an issue.

When you lose as much as the Brooklyn Nets have, it all feels like one giant losing streak. To be precise, the Nets have lost three straight games by double figures, allowing at least 120 points in each.

The Orlando Magic also enter Friday looking to bounce back. They surrendered a 17-point lead in a 121-116 loss to the Philadelphia 76ers on Wednesday. Orlando will take a win any way it can get one, especially with Paolo Banchero sidelined by an ankle injury.

Who: Brooklyn Nets (3-11) vs. Orlando Magic (6-8)When: Friday, November 20, 7:30 p.m. ETWhere: Barclays Center, Brooklyn, NYTV/Streaming: YES Network, DAZN

"(We're) headed back out on the road," said Magic guard Jalen Suggs. "This (loss) didn't kill no momentum, but it was a tough one to drop."

Franz Wagner is coming off one of his best all-around performances of the season, and the Nets will have their hands full trying to slow him down.

It continues his onslaught of late. He's now coming off three consecutive games with at least 27 points, and his ability to create for himself and others makes him the engine of the Magic offense.

Friday also brings a personal storyline. Wagner will face his older brother, Moritz, for the first time as opponents after the latter spent the first five seasons of Franz's NBA career as his teammate in Orlando before signing with Brooklyn this summer.

"It's definitely going to be a little weird," Franz said. "We've obviously played in practice against each other. But I look at it as a huge blessing to be able to share the court together at this level. I'm really happy for him in the situation that he's in. I think me, Moe and the rest of the family will really enjoy the game as much as anything."

Oh yeah and it's an NBA Cup Game. We'll see if that means anything.

#2986. Average Four Factor Index Power Rankings: 2026-2027 NBA Season Week 5

After another week of play (which has amounted to 3-4 additional games for most teams), the young season is rounding into form, and the aFFI rankings are coming into sharper focus. In particular, the data are beginning to suggest the emergence of clear tiers of contention, though there are also a handful of teams that are moving rapidly up or down the list. Let's dig in to the numbers!

Regarding the Silver and Black, the last week has seen some exceptional performances (e.g., against Golden State) balance against statistically unremarkable wins and one clunky loss to Detroit. Figure 1 (below) shows that this combination has resulted in the Spurs staying put in fourth place, though the margin separating the top five positions is razor thin (more on that later). San Antonio's overall aFFI value continues to be weighed down by an ∆EFG% value (54.72) that – while above average – is well below almost any other team that is considered a legitimate contender. Even so, the Spurs are doing quite well with respect to the other three differentials, with particularly excellent average percentiles in ∆ORB% (65.81) and ∆FTM/FGA (66). In summary, the Spurs excellent start to the season has leaned heavily on their ability to protect the ball, rebound, and get to the free throw line relative to their opponents.

Stepping back to review the league as a whole, one striking finding in Figure 1 is that there are a number of teams that have risen or fallen rapidly in the last week. Here are some exceptional standouts:

Memphis Grizzlies (↑8) – After Memphis started the season 6-0, they went on a three-game losing streak and seemed destined to be a promising young team that was ultimately unready to become a true contender. However, they have been on an absolute tear recently, riding exceptional effective field goal differentials (their average ∆EFG% percentile of 70.87 is by far the best in the league) to the top of the aFFI rankings. The Grizzlies continue to have some serious flaws (e.g., a dreadful average ∆TOV% percentile), but they are undoubtedly a team to watch.

Miami Heat (↑5) – Miami's 0-4 start was cringeworthy, and they have a long way to go to join this season's truly elite teams. Having said that, their current six-game win streak has included some impressive wins, and their aFFI value has risen above league average due to some excellent average ∆TOV% (60.24) and ∆FTM/FGA (61.58) percentiles.

Golden State Warriors (↓9) – The young season has already been quite a roller coaster ride for the Warriors. They started slow, but went on a hot streak driven in part by some exceptional performances by rookie Yaxel Lendeborg. Unfortunately, that magic has not continued with Steph Curry out of the lineup, and Golden State is currently on a five-game skid that includes four blowout losses.

Brooklyn Nets (↓8) – Let me be clear here: The Nets' aFFI value was never good; however, one week ago it would have been fair to say that Brooklyn was playing like a team that should win about 40% of it's games, even though they were actually winning about half as often. The games played since then suggest that this lukewarm initial assessment was likely a statistical quirk.

Looking at Figure 2 (below), we can see that teams are beginning to separate into distinct aFFI groupings, which provides us the opportunity to classify teams into tiers. At present, I see four tiers, though as time goes on teams that number is likely to rise:

Early Contenders (8 teams) – At present, there are eight teams that have an aFFI value of 56.28 or more, with the 9th-ranked Knicks (53.61) trailing the early contenders by a healthy margin of over 2.5 points. The first month of play suggests that any of these teams could emerge as true championship contenders as the season goes on, though if we're being honest this group could probably be subdivided further. In particular, the top five teams (Memphis, Minneapolis, Houston, San Antonio, and Boston) are noticeably ahead of everyone else and very close to each other, with the aFFI gap between the first-ranked Grizzlies and fifth-ranked Celtics (1.44 points) being just a bit larger than the gap between Boston and the sixth-ranked Lakers (1.21 points).

Likely Playoff/Play-in Participants (8 teams) – This is an interesting group for several reasons. First, it includes teams that are clearly heading in different directions. In particular, Dallas and Golden State appear to be at very high risk of falling to the tier below, whereas nearly every other member of this tier has seen their aFFI ranking rise in the last week. Philly remains by far the most interesting team in this set, as their stellar 13-3 record remains far out of step with a much more modest aFFI value (51.9). The 76ers' record will almost certainly move to 14-3 after they play the woeful Bucks tomorrow. However, after that they'll enter a difficult stretch of games that should reveal a lot about their true status.

Downslope to the Abyss (12 teams) – This is most crowded tier, with a correspondingly varied collection of outlooks. However, based on the first month of play, the best that most of these teams can hope for is a spot in the play-in (an opportunity that some of them will undoubtedly get, especially in the East). Within this group, the most interesting is clearly Atlanta, as its current record (9-6) is much more competitive that its 18th-ranked aFFI value (47.63) would suggest. Whether or not the Hawks can continue to beat the odds will be of particular interest to the Spurs, who are owed Atlanta's pick in the 2027 draft.

Kings' Corner (2 teams) – The Sacramento Kings and Milwaukee Bucks have collectively played 30 games, yet possess just two wins between them (one for each team). With their season over before the end of November, Bucks fans are already turning toward implausible draft and trade scenarios that will rescue them from obscurity. In stark contrast, Kings fans forgot long ago that any other state of being was possible.

Data Source: The underlying data used for this analysis was collected from Basketball Reference.

#2985. 49ers’ optimism grows for Mike Evans’ return in massive Vikings game

The 49ers might have one of their best offensive players back after a lengthy absence Sunday night against the Vikings in Mexico City.

Receiver Mike Evans was expected to be a limited participant in practice for the second day in a row on Friday and listed as questionable, 49ers coach Kyle Shanahan told reporters, giving him a chance to be available for one of the biggest games of the regular season.

Mike Evans (5) of the San Francisco 49ers runs on the field against the Washington Commanders at Levi's Stadium in Santa Clara, Calif., Oct. 19, 2026. Getty Images

"He had a good day of practice yesterday. As always, he's a little sore today but going in the right direction," Shanahan said.

Evans has missed San Francisco's last three games after leaving near the end of the first half of the Oct. 19 win over the Commanders with Achilles tendinitis. Yet despite missing the future Hall of Famer, the 49ers have scored 38 and 46 in their last two games in wins over the Raiders and Cowboys, respectively.

Shanahan was asked what kind of boost Evans would give the offense if he could return against the Vikings' vaunted defense that enters the weekend ranked second in the NFL in EPA per pass attempt and first in quarterback pressure rate, according to Next Gen Stats.

"A big one," Shanahan said. "Having Mike out there, everyone knows how good Mike is. So I think the guys have done a good job of holding it down without him out there. But Mike's our guy and a very unique player, been doing it at a high level for a very long time and played really unbelievable in the games he played in this year. It'd be great to get him back."

Shanahan spoke before practice and noted Evans would be limited after practicing for the first time since his injury on Thursday with the team in Colorado Springs, Colo., preparing for the altitude at Estadio Azteca in Mexico City.

NFL Network's Ian Rapoport said the 49ers are optimistic Evans can play on Sunday night. Though he compared Evans' injury to what running back Christian McCaffrey dealt with in 2024, when he missed the first eight games due to bilateral Achilles tendinitis.

Mike Evans (5) of the San Francisco 49ers runs after a catch against the Washington Commanders in Santa Clara, California, Oct. 19, 2026. Getty Images

"It feels like to me there's a really good chance," Rapoport said on "The Pat McAfee Show." "It's a tough injury. When Christian McCaffrey had it, and obviously, he had it on both feet, both legs. So a little bit different. But I don't think anyone saw McCaffrey being out as long as he did.

"So it's a tough injury [for Evans], and I don't really want to assume because you never know how it's going to react. But there is optimism that he goes this week. And that would be pretty significant. If he could go, and this is an issue that's in the past for him, not something he's going to continue to worry about, then a very, very good receiver joins a very, very good team. I think we were kinda wondering, how would this 49ers' offense be without him? And they looked pretty good. So adding him to the mix would only help."

#2984. Cathedral tops Bloomington South football in semi-state battle for ages

INDIANAPOLIS — There was no doubt in their minds, they were going for the win.

It was how the whole game had been played on both sides. Bloomington South and Cathedral football were not bowing to the will of the other.

So after Jonathan Winstead made one last spectacular catch at the 2-yard line and Duncan Combs spiked the ball with 3 seconds left, Combs got under center, but Cathedral called timeout. Head coach Gabe Johnson and his seniors had a decision to make down three — go for the tie, or go the glory of a long-awaited final four win.

The offense went back out. The ball was snapped to Combs, who pitched to senior running back Hayden Clark on his right, but Cathedral clogged all the escape routes and began celebrating after a hard-fought 24-21 win in their IHSAA Class 5A Semi-state game at Arsenal Tech on Friday, Nov. 20.

More: Scott Fischer steps down as Edgewood football coach

"He asked us, 'Do we got it?' I believe in our team, I said, 'Yes, let's go for it.'" Combs said. "To come up short by a yard sucks, but I wouldn't have had it any other way.

"Either go out trying your best to win it all, or lose hard with it. It's tough."

The heartache and tears were real. South (10-3) was making a fourth straight semi-state appearance, and none were as close as this one. South could taste it when they broke the sideline huddle and lined up at the 2.

"(The field goal) was for a moment," Johnson said. "But when you have 11 guys who say, 'Let's go for it. Let's go win it.' Then you've got to do it.

"I thought we were going to score. We had momentum at the at point."

"We just wanted an opportunity to play for a state title," Johnson said. "It was the right call to go for it and we didn't get it. There were a lot of calls I think I made that maybe didn't contribute to us being in a better position tonight. That's on me. But our kids fought hard and we gave ourselves to play for a state championship."

Cathedral will now get a chance to add to its haul of 14 as the ghosts of 1998 live on for South.

"They're hurting right now," Johnson said. "There nothing I can tell them to make it feel better. It just has to work its way through your body and leave. It's going to take some time because I know they wanted it badly. And they gave everything they had against a great football team."

The game was everything to be expected from the quality of teams on the field.

"They're a really great team and I think they would say the same about us," Combs said. "When two great teams come together, that's what happens, you get great football. I'm sure they'll do well at state."

Easton Bellini's third-down sack forced Cathedral's punt and set up the dramatic finish.

South got the ball back on its own 15 and needed a fourth-and-1 conversion by Clark to start the drive. It was everything a three-year senior starting QB dreams of and Combs delivered, going 7-of-8 for 77 yards.

First, 5 yards to Connor Parker, 17 to Winstead, 13 to Mayson Lewis, 3 to Clark then 17 to Parker, who just drug his left to the 18-yard line with 13 seconds left. Then 16 to Winstead at the 2.

"Our defense did a great job of holding them there," Combs said. "And when we got the ball, we only had one thing to do and that was move the ball down the field.

"I put faith in all my guys, my O-linemen, my receivers and we did a great job marching down there. I appreciate the coaches for putting faith in us for going for it on the 1-yard line."

Cathedral's starting offensive line goes 275, 285, 290, 310, 330 pounds and leaned on it well, rushing for 155 yards.

But the Irish also freely use junior QB Luke Finn (7-10, 102 yards passing) and sophomore QB Emmett Knowles (13-17, 138) interchangeably throughout the game. Knowles' live arm gave some pep to the passing attack, though both tossed for TDs critical to the win.

The first came after South punted with 1:21 left in the first half. Starting at his own 28, Finn hit a big 48-yard completion and connected on two more passes before finding Ollie Lewis open for a 5-yard score that made it 14-7 at halftime.

"That was not a great series for us," Johnson said.

Cathedral responded in quick fashion again in the third, after Mayson Lewis flew 99 yards with the second half kickoff to tie it.

All night, both teams took chances, going for it on fourth down in their own territory but this time, it backfired spectacularly for the Panthers, who gave the ball back on their own 34.

Next snap, Lewis, a thorn in South's side all night with 11 catches for 119 yards, was wide open and just like that, it was 21-14 Irish.

"I did not like my call to go for it on fourth down there," Johnson said. "I felt good that we could get a yard or two and we didn't."

Cathedral had its own fourth-down letdown when Finn was stopped at the Irish 44. But South couldn't take advantage. The Irish mixed run and pass well in a 14-play drive that started at their own 20 and ended with a 25-yard field goal and a 24-14 edge.

South stormed back, with Clark sneaking out of the back field for two big completions, the last of 30 yards, to score with 6:22 left.

"It was a great group of seniors," Johnson said. "It's as good a four-year span as there's been at Bloomington South minus the state championships.

So, it says a lot for them. It's completely on them. They put in the work."

It was getting tougher for Johnson to hold back tears. South went 29-10 over the past three years thanks in part to this year's seniors.

"We knew we had a great chance coming into this week," Combs said. "And leave it all out there on the field. We've been brothers for years now, going back to middle school, elementary, I've known these dudes my whole life. And I wouldn't want to do it with any other class."

#2982. Is a bottom in for the Nasdaq-100? Charts to watch

(See the video above for Frank's complete chart breakdown.)

Is the Nasdaq-100's recent bounce a sign that the bottom is in? We're going to look at several charts to help answer that question.

First, let's remember how we got here. In September, the NDX broke out from a bullish cup-and-handle pattern, producing a 33,200-upside target. That breakout quickly failed, however, and gave way to a bearish pattern that broke down aggressively two weeks ago.

The decline brought the index relatively close to its 26,350 downside target before the current rebound began.

Zooming out, it's telling to see where the bounce originated. Three important forms of support converged in the 25,000-26,000 area:

This confluence of support helped encourage buying after a roughly 15% decline.

For perspective, we also see what larger drawdowns of 20%, 30%, 40%, and 50% would look like … a lot worse.

The following chart tracks NDX drawdowns from 52-week highs going back to just after the financial crisis. Only a handful of declines exceeded 20%, and three produced relatively quick reversals: late 2018, the Covid lows in 2020 and April 2025.

Historically, buying into declines of approximately 15%–25% has generally paid off, even when additional downside occurred first (yellow highlights).

The chart also highlights previous declines of 10%–16% in green, putting the current pullback into perspective.

The major exception was 2022, when the NDX eventually declined nearly 36% over approximately 10 months ... a much worse outcome, which many investors are concerned could happen again now.

That raises the key question: Are we experiencing another routine correction, or the early stages of a larger bear market?

One important clue will be how far this bounce progresses.

Any rally that fails to reclaim the previous highs could establish a significant lower high. Additional lower highs would provide stronger evidence of a developing, longer downtrend.

We saw this in March 2022, when that first sharp rebound effort failed to reach the previous high before a much more severe decline followed.

The bottom line: The current bounce is encouraging, particularly given where it originated and knowing how well buying prior 10-20% drawdowns has paid off.

From here, it's all about the follow through.  We don't need to see new highs right away, but any rally from here that's met with another wave of strong selling could reignite downside momentum.

Prior to starting CappThesis, he spent 25 years on Wall Street as an equity sales trader, technical analyst, research sales specialist and desk strategist.

#2981. Rangers Acquire Max Sasson From Canucks To Fill Bottom-Six Center Role

The New York Rangers acquired Max Sasson from the Vancouver Canucks on Friday in exchange for a 2029 fourth-round pick.

After losing Joe Veleno due to a lower-body injury on Oct. 4 after just four regular-season games, the Rangers have been searching for another bottom-six center, with a rotating cast of centers filling in Veleno's spot on the fourth line.

Sasson fits the criteria the Rangers are looking for.

Over his three seasons with the Canucks, Sasson has recorded 21 goals, 13 assists, and 34 points in 119 games while averaging 11:27 minutes.

The 26-year-old center has experience as a penalty killer and provides an element of speed to the Rangers' bottom-six forward unit.

During the offseason, the Rangers signed Veleno to a one-year, $1.2 million contract, and he helped balance out the lineup during his four games before landing on injured reserve, as he took on important defensive responsibilities.

Veleno resumed skating on his own on Friday, which Sullivan views as a step in the right direction.

#2980. Anthropic raises $93.5 billion total as underwriters scoop up ‘greenshoe’ overallotment

Anthropic underwriters have officially exercised their overallotment of shares in the historic initial public offering, bringing the total raised to $93.5 billion, according to a release Friday.

The artificial intelligence company raised $81.3 billion on Wednesday and netted an additional $12.2 billion through the overallotment, surpassing SpaceX, which held a record IPO in June.

Anthropic said it sold an aggregate of 1,127,000,000 shares of its Class A common stock at $83 per share in the offering. It sold 980 million shares on Wednesday and its underwriters had the option to purchase up to an additional 147 million shares by Friday as part of the overallotment, which is commonly referred to as the "greenshoe."

Shares of the Claude maker opened trading at $111.50 on the Nasdaq on Thursday, but slid throughout the day and closed at $97.17. The stock sank 12% in premarket trading on Friday during a broad tech sell-off.

In addition to the sale of its Class A common stock, Anthropic closed a concurrent sale of its Class N common stock after a group of tech companies, including Advanced Micro Devices, Google, Microsoft, Nvidia and others, agreed to participate in a $26 billion private placement at the IPO price. Anthropic said Friday that aggregate gross proceeds from its IPO, including the private placement, totaled $119.5 billion, before deducting offering expenses.

Anthropic dodged an unforgiving series of obstacles on its road to the Nasdaq on Thursday, as investors brooded over Jensen Huang's shocking ouster at Nvidia, voters' stinging rebuke of the tech during the midterms and whether rising interest rates would stymie industry plans for a multitrillion-dollar AI infrastructure buildout.

CEO Dario Amodei co-founded the company in 2021 after he and a handful of other researchers, including his sister Daniela, quit their jobs at OpenAI, now Anthropic's chief rival. Anthropic is best known for developing a family of AI models called Claude, which have captivated the corporate world and fueled the company's historic rise.

The siblings celebrated the IPO as a "milestone" in an interview with CNBC on Thursday, but said there's more work ahead for Anthropic.

"For us, it's like one stop on the journey," Daniela Amodei said. "We think about this as, 'Wow, this is an incredible moment for Anthropic for our mission, but we're going to wake up tomorrow and we're going to be the same company.'"

#2979. NBA trade rumors 2026-27: Dejounte Murray update (or, non-update), Kyrie Irving, Trey Murphy, more

Don't expect any NBA trades before Dec. 15, when many of the players who signed new contracts or extensions this summer become eligible to be traded. Even then, it's more likely we'll get closer to the February trade deadline before we see any notable names moved. That includes the guy in the sub-headline below.

While the insatiable NBA rumor mill keeps trying to find clickable updates on Dejounte Murray trades — "The Pelicans talked to the Suns!!!" — the reality is there is no traction toward any kind of deal for the New Orleans guard. Grant Liffman and Chris Mannix break it down well in the video above, but here is the latest.

• Murray, through his agents at Klutch Sports, made a trade request, and the Pelicans publicly acknowledged that, saying, "We will evaluate potential trade opportunities that are in the best interest of our organization." New Orleans hasn't found that yet. Meanwhile, Murray's body language on the court betrays how unhappy he is, and he is shooting 29.6% from 3-point range this season, which doesn't help his trade value.

• To be blunt, teams around the league view Murray as a negative value contract. Those are hard to trade. The fact he is making $32.8 million this season and has a $30.8 million player option for next season also gives teams pause. He is viewed as a 30-year-old guard who's shot 41.9% overall and 30% from 3 in the last two-plus seasons.

• While Murray's camp seems to continue to push the Suns as a destination, league sources have told NBC Sports the Suns have minimal interest, unless the Pelicans want to attach sweetener, such as a first-round pick. That's been reported a lot of other places as well, it's no secret. While the Suns could use a point guard and have done background work on Murray (according to Brian Windhorst of ESPN), they aren't sold on Murray being the All-Star player he was five years ago, and he hasn't looked the same coming off his torn Achilles. How much interest the Pelicans have in Jalen Green should be another question.

• When teams do call the Pelicans, they are more likely to ask about Trey Murphy III and Herb Jones. But Joe Dumars is keeping the price ridiculously high — the Pelicans turned down a trade that would have netted them at least two first-round picks for Murphy over the summer, Michael Scotto of Hoopshype reports.

• Jake Fischer of The Stein Line reports the Suns are more interested in adding a big man with Mark Williams out for the season.

• Utah is one of the teams giving "internal consideration" a potential Kyrie Irving trade, if (or, more likely when) the Mavericks start listening to offers, reports Ian Begley of SNY.TV.

It's worth noting that teams have internal discussions about a lot of players and deals that they never even bother to reach out to the other team about in a serious manner, and where Irving fits in the young timeline in Utah is a good question, but the rumor is out there.

• Multiple reports have coach Brian Keefe's seat in Washington getting hot. This was a team that made moves for veterans in Anthony Davis (playing well) and Trae Young (not playing well) to try and win now, and with the team off to a 5-11 start, someone may be the scapegoat. Washington should have just pivoted to building around AJ Dybantsa, but they are going to be stuck with that horrible four-year, $212 million extension they gave Young this summer for a while. That is the most untradable contract in the league right now.

• Multiple reports have teams interested in trading for Bulls guard Tre Jones. Here is what Zach Lowe said on his Ringer podcast: "Teams are already calling the Bulls about Tre Jones... if he's not the best backup point guard in the NBA, he's very close. If they trade him, they're gonna get real stuff."

#2977. Trump says he discussed Jensen Huang with Xi; Bessent tells CNBC Nvidia chip export policy is unchanged

President Donald Trump on Friday said he and Chinese leader Xi Jinping discussed their shared affinity for former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang during their talks in China this week. Earlier in the day, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said the ousted leader was not mentioned in separate talks he had with Chinese officials.

"I did discuss that with President Xi, and he said, 'Oh, brilliant, brilliant! He's a brilliant man.' He is," Trump said at a White House press conference.

Asked earlier Friday on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" if Huang, a close ally of Trump's, had come up in "any of your discussions with the Chinese," Bessent said "no," without elaborating.

A senior Trump administration official later said that both Trump's and Bessent's remarks were correct, and that both were addressing their own conversations. The official spoke on condition of anonymity to discuss those conversations.

Trump also reiterated that he would like to see Huang — who was replaced by interim CEO Colette Kress after resigning amid allegations of an extramarital affair with a Chinese spy — be restored as Nvidia's CEO.

"I would love to see Jensen be given a second chance," Trump said. "You know, we're a country of second chances, and I'd love to be able to see him. That's a very important company to us, and he put us in a position where we are literally 10 years advanced over other companies."

Nvidia's board is not considering bringing Huang back, a person familiar with the matter told CNBC earlier this week.

The president's comments came amid growing scrutiny of advanced U.S. chips, including those made by Nvidia, ending up in China either legally or by being diverted via third countries.

Both Trump's and Bessent's remarks are potentially an indication that the Trump administration may not focus on cracking down on China's efforts to acquire advanced chips.

Bessent suggested that U.S. policy allowing the sale of Nvidia's powerful H200 AI chips to China remains unchanged following the talks in Shenzhen this week.

"We've already agreed that the ... H200s are the threshold level of chips that could be sold to the Chinese, and, you know, that continues as planned," Bessent said.

"I wouldn't anticipate any change in the policy," he added after CNBC's Sara Eisen noted that Nvidia said it had stopped selling H200s to China.

The administration ignoring the subject of Huang's affair with a possible Chinese spy — whose Singapore-based company Megaspeed has been accused of acting as a cutout for Beijing — contrasts with the importance Trump has placed on beating China in the race to artificial intelligence dominance. It could suggest that despite his support for the U.S. AI industry, Trump is less concerned with addressing China's ongoing efforts to acquire U.S. semiconductors.

Trump has also defended Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, who has come under fire from multiple directions for reportedly slow-walking export restrictions on Chinese chip sales. "I understand both sides of it," Trump said on Monday when he also said he's like to see Huang back as CEO.

Bessent's appearance on CNBC came after Trump's return from a tour through Asia that saw him meet with Xi and Russian President Vladimir Putin in Shenzhen, China.

The meeting was historic as the first time the leaders of the three countries had met without other nations' counterparts. But the trilateral summit appeared to yield few deliverables on any number of the most pressing global issues related to the countries, including the Ukraine and Iran wars or the ongoing AI race.

Bessent told CNBC on Friday that there was a "robust discussion" between Trump and Xi on trade policy and AI.

They "agreed that humans should always be involved in any military decisions that SI makes, and the SI should not be allowed to be autonomous for major military actions," he said, abbreviating the term "super intelligence," Trump's attempted rebrand of AI.

In a CNBC appearance last week, Bessent lamented the "leakage" of U.S.-made AI chips to China and blamed the Commerce Department, led by Lutnick, for slow-walking export controls on suspected Chinese cutouts like Megaspeed.

Multiple Trump administration leaders have long sought to impose export restrictions on Megaspeed, but Commerce "has the pen" and "you will have to ask them about their internal processes," Bessent said in that interview.

On Friday, however, Bessent was much more tight-lipped.

Asked if there is a dispute between him and Lutnick over how aggressively the U.S. should be pursuing Chinese export controls, Bessent said, "I think we're all committed to following President Trump's instructions."

"I think we are going to see that the ... proper chip restrictions are in place, and the remote access has been curtailed," he added. Commerce recently took steps to close one of China's paths to accessing AI processors.

Correction: Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent spoke to CNBC's "Squawk on the Street." A previous version of this story misstated the name of the show.

Editor's note: This story and headline have been updated following additional information provided by the Trump administration about the nature of Bessent's and Trump's meetings in China.

#2976. Warsh wants Fed officials to talk less. Instead, they’re talking more

In his first six months as Federal Reserve chairman, Kevin Warsh has diligently avoided signaling where he sees monetary policy and interest rates headed. His colleagues have been only too happy to fill the void.

Fed officials have been rushing to the microphones lately, voicing sometimes conflicting opinions on how the central bank should be handling an inflation bug that the economy can't seem to shake.

Most agree that rates should be raised — and soon. Some are willing to hike at a measured pace as conditions play out, while others, such as Governor Christopher Waller and St. Louis Fed President Alberto Musalem, entertain the thought of more aggressive hikes in a shorter period of time.

Largely on the sidelines in the debate is Warsh, who has expressed disdain for that sort of forward guidance and argued that it handcuffs policymakers long before they actually have to make a decision. But that hasn't stopped most of the other 18 officials who sit in on Federal Open Market Committee meetings.

In fact, according to a Bank of America analysis, there has been more so-called Fedspeak in the past two weeks than in any similar period since the start of 2025.

"Warsh's refusal to guide markets might be encouraging the rest of the FOMC to speak more, not less," Bank of America economist Aditya Bhave said in a note Thursday.

"There is always a range of views across the 19 FOMC participants. Typically, the Chair would weigh in, indicating to markets whether the hawks or doves are in ascendence," he added. "Absent this leadership, however, both sides might feel incentivized to speak more often and more forcefully."

The increasingly vocal debate comes at a critical time for the Fed and has myriad policy implications.

Bhave noted "a deepening divide" on the FOMC. The Fed's decision not to increase its benchmark rate at its October meeting "increasingly looks like a mistake," and as a result "the hawks have been very vocal of late," he wrote. Leading the charge is Waller, who has raised the possibility of hiking by half a percentage point when the committee next meets in December.

Minutes from the October meeting hint at the tension, particularly when it comes to Warsh's refusal to provide guidance.

The summary related that some officials who were looking for a hike in October "could have supported no change in the federal funds rate provided that it was clearly understood by market participants that further tightening was likely" — a statement that points toward the forward guidance that Warsh has insisted the Fed avoid.

The minutes "revealed that some officials favoring a hike would have accepted a hold if the Fed had clearly communicated further tightening," market veteran Ed Yardeni wrote Thursday. "Warsh's communication philosophy may thus be limiting the Fed's flexibility precisely when it needs it most."

Cleveland Fed President Beth Hammack, who voted against the October pause in favor of a quarter-point hike, tiptoed around the forward guidance issue during a recent public appearance.

Prefacing some observations she made about where she sees policy headed, Hammack noted that, "Those are not promises. Those are not commitments. They're not forward guidance, which is a hot-button topic. But what they are are a conditional set of expectations of where I think the economy can go, based on what I'm seeing."

Hammack's comments illustrate a needle that Fed officials have been trying to thread: continuing to be transparent about their thinking without appearing locked into a future policy that changing economic conditions could render obsolete.

Speaking Thursday, Chicago Fed President Austan Goolsbee framed the argument around credibility.

"I am sympathetic to the view that [Warsh] has expressed that let's not be so, in in our language, forward guidance-oriented, saying here is what I think rates are going to be at the next meeting," Goolsbee said, calling such pronouncements "falsely precise."

However, he quickly separated forward guidance from transparency.

"I think central banks should be careful about doing anything that undermines credibility," Goolsbee said. "That said, I also think that it's pretty important [that] each person who sits around that table explain how they see the world. What do you see in business conditions? You don't want to surprise everybody."

Taking a slightly different tack, Philadelphia Fed President Anna Paulson, in a speech Thursday on policy communication, insisted that markets can figure out where policymakers are going without specific guidance so long as they understand how economic developments will influence decisions.

Paulson also defended the variety of voices coming from the Fed.

"But volume is not the same as clarity," she said. "The measure is not how much we say. It is whether we are understood." The mixture of voices "guards against any single communication becoming the only one that matters."

Financial markets have taken the plethora of Fed communications and interpreted it in a fairly hawkish way.

Traders are implying a 100% chance of at least a quarter percentage point hike in December, with a very small probability of a half-point move, according to the CME Group's FedWatch. From there, odds are high for a follow-up increase in January, then perhaps one more before the end of 2027.

While current inflation indicators are running above 3%, compared to the Fed's 2% target, market measures of longer-term inflation have declined.

Five-year forward expectations, as indicated through the Treasury market, are at their lowest since April. That suggests investors could be less concerned about inflation over the longer term, even as policymakers warn that current price pressures remain elevated.

Still, a sclerotic communications strategy from the Fed amid volatile economic and geopolitical currents could prove dangerous ahead.

"The result of this back-and-forth is undue rates volatility in already turbulent times," BofA's Bhave said.

For his part, Warsh has been advocating for a "family fight" atmosphere inside the Fed, arguing that competing viewpoints deserve a full airing as the committee works towards decisions.

"There are benefits to shaping the market's future expectations. Perhaps this is part of the 'family fight' that Warsh keeps referencing in his pressers," said Tom Graff, chief investment officer at advisory firm Facet. "That family fight seems to be happening out in public."

India trails by 352 with all ten wickets standing and a minimum of 41 overs left in the day.

#2972. Data center construction costs soar 13% in 2026

A version of this article first appeared in the CNBC Property Play newsletter with Diana Olick. Property Play covers new and evolving opportunities for the real estate investor, from individuals to venture capitalists, private equity funds, family offices, institutional investors and large public companies. Sign up to receive future editions, straight to your inbox.

The demand for large-scale data centers in the U.S. this year, driven by the need for computing power for artificial intelligence, grew well beyond the supply of powered land and the labor to build these structures. It also pushed timelines and risk for contractors.

All that resulted in costs rising 13% year-over-year to more than twice the global rate, according to a new report from Turner & Townsend, a global project management company. The gain this year was about three times the last ten-year average.

The report looked at over 500 live or recently completed projects in 62 global markets. It also included a survey of 400 experts in the global data center industry.

While there was some stabilization in cost inflation this year, it was not enough to offset the huge growth in not just demand but speed of construction and delivery.

"More aggressive schedules tie to contractors in particular and subcontractors baking in more risk allowances in their pricing," said Paul Barry, North America data center lead for Turner & Townsend. "So whether that's acceleration allowances, whether that's more overtime and labor attraction allowances, even just higher fees for taking on the risk of delivering at a faster pace than what they would traditionally take on."

All of those things, combined with the spike in demand and the strain on the supply chain, translate into higher costs, he added.

Capital expenditure on global data centers is projected to rise from about $800 billion this year to $1.8 trillion per year in 2050, according to a recent report from PwC. The U.S. is expected to capture almost half of all global investment in AI infrastructure, which includes both data centers and chips.

Power is, of course, key to all data centers, and 56% of those surveyed by Turner & Townsend globally said it was a major obstacle to delivery. Just over half said utility connections typically take between one and two years, and almost a third reported timeframes of over two years. Costs for power vary regionally — from $15.01 per watt in Silicon Valley to $10.51 per watt in Dallas, based on a typical 60-megawatt design.As with every type of construction in the U.S., labor continues to be a significant headwind. Just over one quarter of respondents said their contractor capacity, that is, the ability to manage construction backlogs, was sufficient.

Going forward, Barry said he expects costs to moderate somewhat, with cost increases in the 6% to 10% range in the next year. A lot of the major projects have already started or are starting now, so cost risk has been baked into those contracts.

"If this demand continues, then there'll be more continued flow of project starts and so on, but I think we've hit a big surge in 2026, especially of new big major projects that have been procured," said Barry.

#2966. Trump vows to stop U.S. companies from building data centers abroad, threatens 'tremendous tax'

President Donald Trump on Friday said U.S. companies will be banned from building data centers in other countries and will face a "tremendous tax" penalty if they do so.

"We're going to fight very hard for the data centers," Trump said at a White House press conference before announcing the new policy — which apparently came as news to his own staff.

"I don't know if that's a breaking story," Trump said. "I haven't even said that to my people. But I'm telling them now they're here."

Trump's latest push for more data-center construction in the U.S. came despite Americans repeatedly stating — and showing with their votes — that they dislike the structures.

CNBC's All-America Economic Survey found that 64% of registered voters opposed an AI data center within 10 miles of their home, while just 13% of respondents said they would support one.

If Trump follows through on his threat to clamp down on overseas data center construction, he will have to contend with the fact that numerous U.S. tech giants — including Alphabet, Amazon, Microsoft, Meta and Oracle — have already built them in other countries.

International expansion represents a growth opportunity for the top cloud infrastructure providers, which want to serve clients from data centers that are nearby, sometimes to meet regulatory requirements.

Trump said Friday, however, that if companies want to continue building the centers outside the U.S., "they can, but we're going to put a tremendous tax on them."

"We have a lot of places that want them badly, and they're great places. They have energy, and they have everything. They're tremendous assets," Trump said.

Representatives at cloud providers Amazon, Google, Microsoft and Oracle did not immediately respond to requests for comment.

Trump's remarks came a day after Trump met with Chinese President Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin in China for a trilateral summit that appeared to produce few concrete deliverables on an array of pressing global issues.

Xi "congratulated me on how well we're doing" in artificial intelligence, Trump said, using his preferred term "super intelligence," which he said the Chinese president also likes better.

— CNBC's Jordan Novet and Annie Palmer contributed reporting.

#2965. The bull market has a new problem. The 'smart money' is selling

Companies have shifted from buying back shares to issuing them, adding to the supply that the four-year-old bull market must absorb.

"No matter how you measure it, U.S. corporations have changed their behavior over the past two years, switching from repurchasing in 2024 to issuing today," Acadian Asset Management portfolio manager Owen Lamont wrote in a letter to investors this month. "The question you should be asking is: Why is the smart money selling?"

As of late last week, net new issuance of shares equaled 3.1% of total market capitalization, according to a recent Acadian analysis. While net issuance remains below the peaks during the tech bubble and the covid bubble, the analysis shows that the era of repurchases has ended and an era of equity issuance has begun.

And more supply came to the market this week with Anthropic's blockbuster IPO. The offering drew strong enthusiasm initially with the frontier AI laboratory selling shares in its IPO at $83 each, above the expected range and raising $81 billion. But the stock is falling on Friday and taking the broader stock market with it.

Some investors for years have cited the shrinking stock market as a reason why share prices can keep increasing. As companies repurchased stock, it shrunk the number of shares available for trading and so it helped boost prices. There was more money chasing fewer shares. Not to mention, it had the effect of boosting earnings per share by lowering the denominator in that ratio. Now that trend has reversed with increased supply acting as a potential headwind on the bull market.

A 2000 paper by Malcolm Baker and Jeffrey Wurgler found that when companies issued more equity over debt, low market returns followed, suggesting that managers timed the market when issuing securities. The Baker-Wurgler equity share, which measures equity issuance as a share of total stock and bond issuance, peaked at about 18% in 2021. It has risen in recent months but remains below that peak, Lamont wrote.

The Federal Reserve data show a similar trend in the year-over-year change in trailing 12-month gross stock and bond issuance. Debt gross issuance rose first in 2020, followed by equity issuance in 2021. More recently, debt issuance began climbing in 2024, followed by a surge in equity issuance in 2026.

Recent deals show companies tapping equity markets in different ways.

Oracle filed to sell $20 billion in stock through an at-the-market program and issued another $10 billion in common shares in November. In October, Meta priced a $25 billion common-stock offering and $12.5 billion in mandatory convertible preferred shares. Alphabet announced an $84.8 billion equity raise in June to help fund its AI infrastructure buildout. SpaceX raised $85.7 billion in its June IPO after underwriters exercised their overallotment.

"Before 2026, firms were net repurchasers of equity, consistent with the idea that they viewed their shares as undervalued. Today, for whatever reason, firms are issuing equity on net," Lamont wrote.

The investor said he cannot rule out that we are in an AI bubble now.

#2962. Zscaler stock jumps 18% on report that CrowdStrike in talks to buy security vendor

Zscaler shares surged 18% in extended trading on Friday after Bloomberg reported that CrowdStrike is in talks to buy the cybersecurity vendor. Shares of CrowdStrike shares fell more than 8%.

The talks between the two companies are ongoing, Bloomberg reported, citing sources familiar in the matter. CrowdStrike declined to comment on the report. Zscaler didn't immediately respond to CNBC's request for comment.

CrowdStrike has been one of the big cybersecurity winners in the artificial intelligence boom, with its stock about doubling in value this year, lifting its market cap to $235 billion. Zscaler, meanwhile, is down 13% this year, pushing its market cap down to about $32 billion.

Along with CrowdStrike, Palo Alto Networks has seen its business boom as businesses race to upgrade their networks in the AI era. Palo Alto closed Friday at a nearly $282 billion market cap.

Should a deal take place, it would be among the largest in the history of the cybersecurity industry. Google acquired Wiz last year for $32 billion. In 2024, Cisco paid about $28 billion for Splunk. Earlier this year, Palo Alto closed its purchase of CyberArk for $25 billion.

CrowdStrike's deals to this point have been on the smaller side.The company announced plans earlier this month to acquire agentic observability platform Fiddler AI and AI trust platform Dreadnode.

#2961. Miami Hurricanes Rising Star Javian Mallory Signs With New Agent

MIAMI, Fla. – One of the biggest transformations in college football is student athletes having agents.

They negotiate N.I.L. deals and other business inquiries on behald of the client. It's the business side of college sports overall.

It was reported on Friday, then officially announced, that Miami Hurricanes freshman running back Javian Mallory is signing with Eric Duonn of L.A.A. (Loyalty Above All) Sports and Entertainment. Before adding the 19-year-old as a client, the firm represents James Cook (Buffalo Bills; RB), Darrelle Revis (Former NFL cornerback), and J.K. Dobbins (Denver Broncos; RB).

Mallory is one of the most outstanding freshmen in college football this season. He's logged 580 rushing yards (11th in the ACC) on 84 carries while scoring nine touchdowns on the ground (tied for fifth). Even before CharMar "Marty" Brown's season-ending right knee injury against Wake Forest on Sept. 18, Mallory has surpasses other veterans on the depth chart, such as Jordan Lyle and Girard Pringle Jr.

The Fort Lauderdale (Fla.) native has delivered an immediate impact and is on pace to receive freshman All-American honors. Mallory has shown speed, power, and balance. According to Pro Football Focus, he has forced 21 missed tackles.

Mallory has displayed elite production when playing in front of home fans at Hard Rock Stadium. He averages 79.6 rushing yards and two total touchdowns (receiving and on the ground). When asked by MiamiRoundtable about the trend following the Hurricanes 36-25 win over Duke, he responded:

"I came to Miami to put on for the city," he said.

There is no confirmation as to if or how much Mallory is receiving in N.I.L. According to The NIL Standard, he's estimated around $91,000. Given Mallory's production, Dounn will have a strong foundation to negotiate for a bigger deal ahead of the 2027 college football season.

According to LAA's website, over 75 athletes have signed, which has resulted in over 10 years of experience in the agency field and at least $750 million in deals negotiated.

Given senior star Mark Fletcher Jr.'s projection to declare for the 2027 NFL Draft, Mallory could emerge as the Hurricanes' starter in the backfield next year. He will still have to earn it, but this season gives him a head start. Mallory has often received comparisons to five-time Pro Bowler and former program running back Frank Gore from the fan base.

The Hurricanes have reached the final stretch of the regular season, as kickoff is scheduled for 7:00 p.m. E.T. against Virginia Tech on Saturday and will be televised on ESPN.

#2960. A trading strategy to ride out the Nvidia uncertainty and generate income in the process

Jensen Huang's abrupt resignation has sent shockwaves through the tech sector, dragging Nvidia's (NVDA) stock down roughly 30% since Oct. 6. The market despises uncertainty, and losing a visionary founder, particularly one without a well-telegraphed succession plan, makes it worse.

However, for opportunistic investors, this panic has made the stock cheaper (obviously) while options have become significantly more expensive. Trading in the $160s, Nvidia's valuation has taken a big haircut, yet its underlying business fundamentals remain largely intact. While Huang was the architect of Nvidia's AI dominance, the company's moat — superior GPUs, the CUDA software ecosystem, and networking expertise — does not walk out the door with him. The company's structural advantage in the global artificial intelligence rollout persists.

Buying good companies at a discount is investing 101, but the current market dynamics may do something else for those who use options.  Huang's departure guarantees that Nvidia will be operating under a cloud of transition for the foreseeable future. Executing a permanent CEO search while navigating global regulatory scrutiny takes time. Consequently, Nvidia's implied volatility (IV)—the market's pricing of expected future price swings — has spiked to highs not seen since the tariff tantrum in the first half of 2025, and this elevated IV is highly likely to persist for several months as the leadership drama slowly unfolds.

This prolonged period of high implied volatility can be a goldmine for option sellers, specifically those executing options overlays such as covered call strategies. When you buy NVDA shares at these depressed levels and simultaneously sell out-of-the-money call options against your position, the premiums you collect are directly tied to IV. Because fear and transition-related uncertainty have dramatically inflated these option prices, the income generated from selling covered calls is now significantly higher than usual.

This lowers the risk profile of holding the stock considerably over time. Consistently collecting these inflated premiums systematically lowers your overall cost basis in the underlying shares. If the stock trades sideways or recovers slowly during the CEO search, you are generating an outsized cash yield on a stagnant asset. If the stock experiences further downside turbulence, the heavy premium income acts as a substantial downside buffer, meaningfully reducing the strategy's overall risk over time.

By deploying a persistent covered call strategy, investors can leverage the exact fear driving the stock down to generate robust, risk-reducing income while maintaining exposure to the undeniable future of AI.

#2959. Ranking all the scary comments Steph Curry makes in the ESPN article

Three days ago, ESPN's Anthony Slater published a story that served to remind us all that time slows for no one and that Steph Curry's playing days are numbered. It was startling. The specifics of it were so definitive, so obviously instructive of how the music would suddenly stop, that many fans were (rightfully) shook. Every quote feels like a night night dagger; but, since this is a sports blog, let's rank them by order of "BANG!"-itude.

First: if you haven't read the piece, go and read it, because I'll be jumping around.

Is this actually a scary quote? Not totally. We must always remember that a professional athlete who has played at the highest level possible for a human being during this moment in time lives in the moment most of the time. But you know the end is on the horizon once the athlete begins to discuss the end. Most of our stars have been stuck in some version of that kid who loved the game they played and wound up making millions for doing and mere recognition of mortality is revolutionary in terms of self-reflection. But the scare factor is that Steph's aware the party's winding down. His body has been telling him, and now his conscious thought has joined the fray.

This is a heightening of that recognition. A recognition of context, but also "the ability to convince myself" is a heckuva self-reflection. His mind is asking his body to prove it, and — for now — it's satisfying his standards. Will he lower his standards to keep convincing himself? Or does he have a "If I can't do 'x' anymore, then that's it" in mind?

Slater gets quotes from Steve Kerr in this piece, too, and for this segment he relates:

"He's staying off his feet [more often]," Kerr said. "Relying on the fact that he's built up this incredible skill level that's not going to leave him. Then, he's found a way to stay really fit without pounding the knees, the ankles. He's on the bike, underwater treadmill. He's creative."

But I'm putting this 8th because I'm a little scared by how this aging curve seems to have surprised Curry. Slater begins the article by talking about earlier this year, and that being the first time he really seemed to have grappled with his aging body.

Every athlete — every human being! — is different, of course, so this isn't a criticism, it's just a note that Steph Curry is not one of those athletes who plans too far ahead. His greatness has been defined by putting the pedal to the metal at all times; but, everyone in their mid-thirties would be wise to reassess their body maintenance. You can see how the idea of leaving something on the table doesn't appeal to him. It's not a lack of wisdom or intelligence, it's just the way that he is wired. But as a fan reading this, the fear factor is in how he's just going to keep dancing until the music stops.

Well, okay, and then there's an acknowledgement that he needs to slow some aspect of it down. This line definitely amps up the bummer vibes of the piece. It's like he's counting how many sunrises he's got left.

Or counting how many more sandwiches he'll have left to eat in his life. But you can see why Dylan Thomas urged rage because contemplation or otherwise quiet reflection is just a bummer. It sucks to be reminded that this is all going to end — better to burn out than fade away, right? But that's the tension of being a professional athlete. That's certainly the trajectory Steph is on; will this slowdown prolong his career?

This is the first quote of the aptly titled article, "The clock is ticking on Steph Curry's final act with the Warriors." Slater starts with an anecdote about an off-day workout going awry and quickly pulls in the oblique abdominal strain, but the constant throughout the article is Steph's knee.

"a weird soreness and pain that didn't go away"

"It was basically holding. It wasn't going either direction."

There's even discussion of how much it held him back, where he was just going through the motions while on the court at times. This is as close to a medical assessment as we get in the piece, and it's devastating. Cartilage doesn't come back as we age thanks to how blood flow works in the area. That's where things like stem cell therapy come into play. But, at this point, it's pretty clear that after all the miles Steph Curry has logged over the years, we're talking about patching an engine that's getting near the end of its life. Consider this part one of a two-part idea. Part two:

As terrible a setback as the oblique abdominal strain/tear is, this quote plus the one before it really does point to his knee being the ticking time bomb, and that he's well aware that's the case.

The story is that Stephen Curry had the biggest setback of his professional career this calendar year and because his body has started to break down. It doesn't get any scarier than that. This year is almost over. What horror(s) will next year bring?

But I'll end this post … the least scary thing Curry says in the whole piece.

If circumstances demanded it, Curry said he would've been fine sliding into more of a complementary role late in his career, similar to most fading stars. Perhaps he eventually will. "It would not kill my ego," Curry said. "I want to win. Whatever that means, you make decisions based on what's in front of you."

You're not going to find any Stephen Curry followers who don't think the guy has a degree of humility about him. That's part of what makes him special. He might be the greatest. At times, even he might even acknowledge that he's "up there," in terms of the all-time greats. But he's also aware of how fleeting that feeling can be. It won't be long before he's an afterthought. He's certainly been in the league long enough to see opinions, rankings, or simply stature diminish or outright disappear overnight. And there's also the thing where, sometimes, we need to say something out loud just to convince ourselves it's true.

We don't know when Curry will return from his current injury, but there's still just a bit more time left for us to enjoy a bit more history created by his play. If this isn't the last ride, then it's the penultimate one.

#2957. A massive bearish trade just happened in the chip stocks

So much for buying the dip.As the S&P 500 entered correction territory and the Nasdaq-100 flirted with bear-market territory, down 19% from highs as of midday Friday, the single biggest options trade of the day was a near $100-million bet that the VanEck Semiconductor ETF (SMH) has a long way more to fall.Around 12:30 E.T., someone spent $93 million buying 20,000 380-strike puts expiring Sep. 17, 2027, according to trading data reviewed by SpotGamma and Volland, options-analytics platforms that both show the trade as a likely opening position, with volume exceeding existing open interest in the contract. The trade bets that the index will fall below $380, a nearly 8% decline from its current level. The SMH is off 40% from its recent all-time highs.

"That trade stands out big-time," wrote Jason DeLorenzo, founder and owner of Volland. "It is either a hedge or speculative, but either way it isn't a vote of confidence."The puts were bought for $46.50 a piece, an arguably expensive choice for a downside bet with volatility in the ETF trading at 70.

"You're paying a metric ton of Vega for that thing," added SpotGamma's Brent Kochuba, referring to the contract's sensitivity to changes in volatility of the underlying chip ETF. "Maybe they were a forced buyer, or maybe they're actually selling this, which can still be the case even though the price shows it happening closer to the ask."

The semiconductor index headed for a 9% drop this week alone.

#2955. ANALYSIS: Day’Ron Sharpe is emerging as a bright spot despite the Nets’ ugly start

Things aren't pretty in Brooklyn right now, not even close.

The Nets are 3-11, and even though the record looks bad, the way they keep losing adds to the frustration. Brooklyn is coming off an embarrassing 137-107 loss to a Chicago Bulls team that entered the game with just two wins.

Beyond that, the team has now lost five games by at least 15 points this season, the most in the NBA. It doesn't stop there, either. The Nets have allowed 135+ points in back-to-back games for the first time since April 1969!

So, yeah, optimism is pretty hard to come by these days. That's to put it mildly, of course.

But for the fearless, ride-or-die fans out there anxiously searching for something, just anything, to feel good about, there is a bright spot worth discussing. That bright spot goes by the name Day'Ron Sharpe.

Sharpe, 25, is the longest-tenured player currently on Brooklyn's roster, in his sixth season with the organization. After the team dealt Nic Claxton to Chicago over the summer in a three-team deal with the Minnesota Timberwolves to bring in Julius Randle, this is Sharpe's first year as the Nets' full-time starting center.

Throughout his career, analytics have always loved Sharpe, particularly for his offensive rebounding. Entering this season, though, the question was more about whether that efficiency would translate to a larger sample size.

And so far, it has continued, and then some. According to Cleaning the Glass, the Nets have been 18.4 points per 100 possessions better with Sharpe on the floor than without him, placing him in the 93rd percentile among all forwards and bigs who have played a minute this season.

While rebounding has always been his big calling card, the biggest difference in Sharpe's on/off numbers seems to come on offense. The Nets have scored 13.0 more points per 100 possessions with the North Carolina product on the court, which ranks in the 95th percentile. It's also the seventh-biggest discrepancy among forwards and bigs.

For perspective, his overall on/off differential currently ranks ahead of notable names like Nikola Jokić (+15.5) and Alperen Şengün (+15.5). He's also just one percentile beyond Karl-Anthony Towns. All three of those players are averaging over 30 minutes per game, compared to Sharpe's 23.6, but that certainly doesn't make it something to scoff at.

Sharpe is no stranger to ranking high in efficiency categories, but the most encouraging part is that he's doing it with an expanded workload so far. Through 14 games, he is averaging over 23 minutes per night. His career high in a single season before this was last year, when he averaged 18.7 in 62 games. So, the big man has never averaged over 20 minutes per night before this season. He's also started all 14 games for Brooklyn this year, already shattering his previous career-high in starts per season, which was just eight in his rookie campaign.

Overall, Sharpe, who re-signed on a two-year, $20 million contract this offseason, has averaged 8.4 points, 7.1 rebounds (including 3.3 offensive boards), and 4.0 assists on 52.9% shooting.

Another encouraging number is the 4.0 assists per game. With Claxton out of the picture, Sharpe has taken on a much bigger role as the offensive facilitator. He's done a really solid job of embracing the responsibility that comes with being an offensive hub. It's a role Claxton anchored for years in Brooklyn.

That's especially important for a Nets team that still seems to be searching for an identity and consistency on offense. The passing isn't necessarily new, either. Sharpe had flashed that in smaller doses throughout his career. Now that he is getting the opportunites, it's becoming even more obvious.

Against the Atlanta Hawks on November 7, he dropped 13 assists and his first career triple-double. The 13 assists were the most in a single game by a Nets center in franchise history.

For me, the biggest concern regarding Brooklyn's squad entering the year was their lack of rim protection after dealing Claxton. That left a big void, and the Nets didn't do much over the summer to fix it. They added Mo Wagner, but as fans have seen, he is much more of an offensive player and that's being kind. That's not to downplay his impact; his offensive ability is certainly important, but he has limitations down low.

It's never been a strength of Sharpe's either. As a result, the team is currently allowing 48.7 paint points per game, the ninth-most in the NBA. For a team like Brooklyn that lacks elite-level firepower and just plain athleticism, it's difficult to win consistently when you're allowing nearly 50 points per game in the paint.

By no means does all of that responsibility fall on Sharpe, though. Sean Marks deserves criticism for structuring the roster that way, and his frontcourt teammates also haven't necessarily helped turn the tide in that facet.

Ultimately, his start to the year has been very promising. Digging deeper than the sheer numbers, the continued efficiency and success while playing at a career-high rate is exactly why fans should be excited moving forward.

There are still 68 games to play, but so far, Sharpe is making the most of his extended role.

#2954. Clemson commit drills 58-yard field goal, walk-off winner in Florida playoff stunner

Brandin Gallaway has the leg to change a football game. Friday night, the Clemson commit did it twice.

The Palmetto senior kicker drilled a 58-yard field goal to tie the game before halftime, then came through with a 34-yard kick as time expired to give the Tigers a dramatic 12-10 upset of Tampa Bay Tech in the Class 5A-Region 3 semifinals.

Gallaway accounted for all 12 of Palmetto's points, converting all four of his field-goal attempts to send the Tigers (11-1) into the regional final.

His biggest kick of the first half came with 35 seconds remaining. Facing a long attempt, Gallaway connected from 58 yards to tie the game at 6-6 and give Palmetto momentum heading into the break.

Tampa Bay Tech had taken the lead late in the first quarter on a 15-yard touchdown pass from Martavius Conway Jr., but missed the extra point.

Gallaway put Palmetto ahead with a 42-yard field goal with 3:41 remaining in the third quarter. Tampa Bay Tech answered with a 29-yard field goal with four minutes left in the game, setting up a dramatic finish.

The Tigers' ensuing possession stalled near midfield, but their defense gave Gallaway another opportunity.

Palmetto's defense flushed Conway out of the pocket, and Jordan Allen stepped in front of his pass at the Tampa Bay Tech 42-yard line, returning the interception to the 34.

Palmetto moved the ball to the 17-yard line before sending Gallaway onto the field with three seconds remaining.

The senior, like he had all night, delivered once more for safe measure.

His 34-yard attempt sailed through the uprights as time expired, sending Palmetto into the regional final and capping a four-field-goal performance that will be difficult to forget.

Palmetto will travel to face top-seeded Armwood next Friday. Armwood advanced with a 37-12 victory over Sumner.
