#2860. Player grades: SGA's 40 points lead Thunder to 126-120 win over Pelicans

OKLAHOMA CITY — Racing down the floor, Shai Gilgeous-Alexander had his eyes set on the rim. He trucked through Jeremiah Fears to bank in the and-one layup. The two-time MVP walked the baseline and stared at the crowd before he finished the dagger three-point play with 34 seconds left.

The Oklahoma City Thunder survived a thriller in a 126-120 win over the New Orleans Pelicans. This was part of the 2026 NBA Cup West Group B.

In a sport that features plenty of runs, this was one even more roller-coast-esque than usual. The Thunder completely blitzed the Pelicans to get started with a quick 11-0 lead a little over two minutes in. And then New Orleans immediately responded with a 17-4 run. Saddiq Bey buried an outside jumper to cap it off.

The Thunder were in a 36-33 deficit after the first quarter. Once again, it was another NBA opponent that completely cooked OKC from the perimeter. This time, it was by one of the worst outside-shooting teams in the league. The second quarter was much the same story.

As the Thunder fell behind by double-digit points, they couldn't muster up much offense. Pair that with the Pelicans getting whatever they wanted, it was a recipe for bad vibes. Zion Williamson bully-balled his way to the cup. He looked like his vintage self. Jeremiah Fears' speed helped him dissect OKC's defense. The latter only had 23 points in the second frame. It was in a 64-56 deficit at halftime.

Yeesh. The Thunder looked lifeless for most of the first half. For as demoralizing as things have gotten with them, multiply that by 10 for the Pelicans. To finish a putrid first half, Gilgeous-Alexander had a scary-looking fall on a drive to the basket. His shoulder collided with the floor.

To start the second half, the Thunder fell into an even deeper hole. Herbert Jones' back-to-back corner 3-pointers were soul-crushers. That put OKC in a 91-76 deficit. The possibility of dropping another gimme stared them straight in the face once again. They salvaged things with a 14-2 run to end the third frame.

At that point, the Thunder scored 36 points in the third quarter. The offense finally started to play like an upper-half team. Jared McCain and Bennett Stirtz caught on fire. Aday Mara was a godsend as he played like an old-school seven-footer. They were in a 98-92 deficit with a dozen minutes to go. Another clutch-time scenario was on the verge of materializing.

Eventually, McCain erupted the OKC crowd with a game-tying outside jumper. Knotted up at 102 points with nine minutes left, the Thunder played rejuvenated in comparison to their first-half selves. Mara injected some youthful energy as a stop-and-board sequence had him thumping his chest in excitement. When Gilgeous-Alexander checked in, they actually had a three-point lead with eight minutes left — a great spot to be at.

Struggling in the clutch in recent outings, Gilgeous-Alexander finally came through. He drilled a stepback 3-pointer on Williamson's grill. After the latter had a couple of successful downhill drives to the rim, Mara's weak-side defense came up with a big-time stop.

Gilgeous-Alexander closed this out with an and-one driving layup. Meanwhile, the Pelicans' hot outside shooting fizzled out at the worst time possible. The Thunder ran away with this game with 34 points in the final frame. They live to see another day as the alternative of two straight home losses to similar-level teams would've continued uncomfortable conversations about this zombified group.

The Thunder shot 53% from the field and went 14-of-36 (38.9%) from 3. They shot 14-of-20 on free throws. They had 26 assists on 49 baskets. Four Thunder players scored double-digit points.

Gilgeous-Alexander led the way with 40 points and eight assists. Mara had a monstrous 22-point and 16-rebound double-double. McCain dropped 18 points and three rebounds. Stirtz helped with 18 points and two assists.

Meanwhile, the Pelicans shot 45% from the field and went 17-of-39 (43.6%) from 3. They shot 19-of-24 on free throws. Thet had 23 assists on 42 baskets. Six Pelicans players scored double-digit points.

Willamson had a throwback night with 22 points, seven assists and six rebounds. Fears also shone with 22 points, six rebounds and five assists. Bey surprised with 21 points and six rebounds. Trey Murphy III had 17 points and four rebounds in limited action. Derik Queen had 11 points and seven rebounds. Jones finished with 14 points and three assists.

Well, you'll get wins any way you can get them in this Post-D.C.C. (Dub, Chet, Caso) era. The Thunder can't get too picky on how they play out. At this point, it's just about raw survival. Is the defense still concerning? Yes. Is this form of winning a sustainable formula? Probably not. But you can worry about those problems another day. For now, Gilgeous-Alexander and friends did just enough to squeeze by the Pelicans.

That's what these two months are about. And hey — if Mara and Stirtz continue on their current trajectories — maybe this stretch of ugly basketball will be remembered fondly as their respective breakouts. The Thunder badly need an injection of youth and fresh faces. This is one of the few ripple effects of OKC's apocalyptic injury wave that happened a month ago.

Dancing with Williamson, Gilgeous-Alexander had that killer look in his eye. He wanted to add to his clutch-moments catalog — which has seldom seen clips added recently. He finally did so. Going with the patented stepback 3-pointer, he drilled the jumper despite the fellow All-Star player's textbook contest.

Gilgeous-Alexander finished with 40 points on 14-of-24 shooting, eight assists and three rebounds. He shot 4-of-7 from 3 and went 8-of-11 on free throws. He also had one block.

The one-man show continues. Gilgeous-Alexander has fully embraced the task required to keep the Thunder alive. The two-time MVP has dropped video-game scoring numbers after video-game scoring numbers. This time, he complemented the meaty box-score stat line with a win and fourth-quarter highlights. It was a textbook super-efficient 40-ball that one of the greatest players has normalized throughout the decade.

Back to the formula for Gilgeous-Alexander. After adventuring out to the perimeter to start the season, he's returned to his within-the-perimeter bag. You can't outdo the Big Mac sauce at McDonald's. He completely dissected and destroyed New Orleans' nonexistent defense. It's funny. It has a ton of defense-first names on paper. But for whatever reason, it can't actually gel on the floor.

Gilgeous-Alexander exposed that hooping oxymoron. He drilled a bucket of mid-range jumpers. His outside shot even fell for him. And the drives to the rim featured pure shot-making artistry. It was also another game where the free-throw attempts reached double digits. Everything is aligning for him to have another super-efficient 30-plus point campaign for a historic fifth consecutive year.

As the rest of the Thunder needed a half to get going, Gilgeous-Alexander kept them at bay. He had 19 points in the first half. Even when everybody else discovered a second-half groove, his scoring onslaught continued. He finally played closer with nine points in the final frame. The Pelicans didn't have a counterpunch as several role players took turns jacking up outside shots.

Been discussed ad nauseam, but this is what the Thunder will need out of Gilgeous-Alexander if it wants a puncher's chance most nights. That is, until Chet Holmgren returns. Against a disoriented Pelicans, having a 40-burger felt like a given. He's back to having those on a regular basis. The NBA superstar has done his fair share of carrying jobs this year. This was the latest example.

Winning the easy jump ball over Fears, Mara rolled to the rim. Kenrich Williams played a quick game of catch. The 21-year-old's trust in his veteran teammate's savviness paid off. He had a wide-open dunk to give the Thunder a high-leverage bucket in the final 90 seconds.

Mara finished with 22 points on 11-of-15 shooting, 16 rebounds and two assists. He also had two blocks.

With each passing game, Mara has seen his NBA confidence balloon. Alongside that, his play as well. This was the peak of the first month of his rookie year. He notched a double-double that has never happened before in Thunder history. The 7-foot-3 giant was a one-man dunk machine as nobody on the Pelicans' frontcourt dared to meet him at the rim.

The Thunder didn't take long to realize how easy it was going to be to slice up the Pelicans' paint defense. Isaiah Hartenstein connected on Mara for a couple of easy dunks to get things going. The latter had a dozen points in the first half. It wasn't just all spoon-fed stuff either, though. He showcased his old-school-esque footwork in the post. Each time he scored, his confidence oozed through the TV screen.

Patient in the post, Mara has mastered the art of the pump-fake and pass-fake. He didn't even need to depend on his raw strength and size to get the best of his defenders. Looking like prime Al Jefferson, the 21-year-old was a scientist in the paint. It was all finesse as he feathered in a few layups.

On the boards, Mara was a monster. On both ends. Remember when that was a concern in the NBA draft process? What makes this even more impressive is that the Pelicans are filled with plenty of talented frontcourt players. As the Thunder try to stitch together a decent NBA offense, his second-chance looks and putbacks have proven to be a lifeblood.

I mean, Mara has completely salvaged OKC's year. I don't think it's an overreaction to say that. Peaks like tonight write for themselves. But he's low-key had a steady floor game. There's a reason why the Thunder have settled on the Mara-Hartenstein frontcourt look for their starting lineup in this stretch. It's been a minute since OKC has seen fresh faces help at this level. Incredibly timely for it to happen right now.

Getting a one-on-one look with Williamson, Stirtz went to work. He shuffled his feet toward the baseline. The 23-year-old boasted his self-creation talent as he drilled a mid-range jumper. Also knocking down his catch-and-shoot looks, this was a sneak peek of his best-case scenario.

Stirtz finished with 18 points on 7-of-10 shooting, two assists and two rebounds. He shot 4-of-6 from 3. He also had one steal.

Since the Post-D.C.C. era started, the Thunder desperately needed complementary scorers. Stirtz had fit the bill on paper. But this was one of the early times that he materialized that over to the court. Considering how short his leash had been this year, OKC decided to let him get creative and gain confidence as a bucket-getter.

The result? Stirtz's best game of the year. He had a couple of straight-line drives to the rim. But most of his buckets happened in the jumper-variety. The outside shot fell for him. He drained his first high-leverage outside shot of his career in the final four minutes of this contest to put some distance on the scoreboard.

Like Mara, Stirtz has been a contributor from the jump — albeit on rockier waters and in a smaller role. But the Thunder are hoping he can eventually enter a flow state as a scorer. Right now, he's still adjusting to the NBA. To the point that OKC hinted at wanting him to play much more within the flow instead of overthinking things. This tape should be their top example of why they believe that's for the best for him.

Pump-faking from the outside, McCain took Bey to the cup. A step-through move helped him get his first bucket of a banked layup. That was enough to turn into a microwave scorer. He swished a deep catch-and-shoot outside jumper soon after. Then did the same a couple of minutes later to open the second half.

McCain finished with 18 points on 7-of-14 shooting and three rebounds. He shot 3-of-9 from 3 and went 1-of-2 on free throws. He also had one steal and one block.

The most Jekyll-and-Hyde player on the Thunder had the most Jekyll-and-Hyde performance. First half? Zero points. Scoreless. Second half? A full 18. As OKC scored in a flurry, he aided with his sizzling shot-making. He had a dozen points in the third quarter. That type of scoring run is what makes him such a fan favorite and tantalizing prospect.

The outside jumper finally fell. Most importantly, though, McCain put up outside shot attempts. That has surprisingly been a struggle this year. Too often, he's settled for mid-range looks. But if he's going to thrive in the NBA, he needs to take most of his attempts from deep. And this game is why as he turned it around with a surging second half.

Sure, McCain's scoreless halves happen too often. His highs and lows are too volatile and badly need to center his consistency. But he salvaged this outing as he helped the Thunder get a comeback win. Until help arrives, they're going to continue to rely on his hot-or-cold scoring game. That's really one of the few options they have sans Gilgeous-Alexander.

#2859. 3 thoughts as the Mavericks face another winnable matchup at home against the Los Angeles Clippers

The Dallas Mavericks (8-7) remain an interesting case study in the topsy-turvy NBA ahead of Saturday's clash with the Los Angeles Clippers (4-10), and their own penchant for wild swings in performance level are only part of that story.

They've got significant injuries holding them back in some ways, but that comes against the backdrop of players like Zaccharie Risacher and Caleb Martin, who are enjoying various levels of resurgence this year. Kyrie Irving is back and generally playing well, but how back is he really, and how committed are the Mavericks to seeing Irving finish the season with the team? Naji Marshall is playing the role in terms of minutes of a guy who just got extended for three years after setting a career-high scoring mark, but he's averaging just 10.9 points and 2.8 rebounds per game in November, so what gives there? Marshall seems to simply be the lesser evil right now in the mid-off between himself and Max Christie.

The stew is pungent and rich, and it should not be overlooked that, despite the mounting question marks, an 8-7 record is at least one step ahead of schedule as we near the season's quarter pole. The best part about that record is the chance to make it 9-7 against another vulnerable opponent at American Airlines Center on Saturday.

The Clip Show this year is a Darius Garland-Brook Lopez-Rui Hachimura production, with a little Keaton Wagler and Derrick Jones Jr. sprinkled in thus far. The 38-year-old Lopez, in particular, averaging 15.4 points on better than 55% shooting from the floor, is an inspiration for all of us who are past our prime. He's scored 23 or more in three of the Clippers' last four games and 20 or more in five of the last seven. That includes games against the San Antonio Spurs (23) and the Oklahoma City Thunder (26). He' shooting lights out from 3-point range — nearly 45% to start the year.

And Lopez isn't the only Clipper doing work from long range this season. LA is second in the NBA in team 3-point percentage, at 39.2%. Hachimura and Jones Jr. are also hitting about two per game from deep on surprisingly solid shooting clips. Jones' 50% mark on 3.7 attempts per game thus far this year will likely regress, but he's been feeling it from out there to say the least. Max Strus, Kriss Dunn, Gradey Dick and Wagler are all shooters as well.

But yet, they're 4-10. They don't rebound well, they turn the ball over the second most (17.6 per game) in the NBA and they rank 25th in defensive rating (117.2). A tale as old as the peach-basket net, really. Irving and Cooper Flagg should be able to have their way with this bunch, as they did on Wednesday against the flailing Golden State Warriors.

Mavericks first-year head coach Dusty May was asked on Friday if the team is close to having center Dereck Lively II back in action. He gave a very Clintonesque answer, questioning the technical definition of the word "close," which is never a good sign. Yes, Bill, er, Dusty, the whole question does center around what the definition of the word "is" is.

"I asked the same question this morning & close is a term that was used by D-Live," May said, according to the Dallas Morning News. "And when you look at it from the magnitude that he's been out for a while. He's doing a lot more now, but close, I'm not sure what close is. I would have to defer to our medical staff on that. He's in great spirits. He's moving well. I don't know what the exact ramp-up and build-up is, but he's progressing."

"I'm not sure what close is," is doing a lot of heavy lifting in that answer. Close is close. Can you put a number on the number of days or weeks away the player is? No? Ok, then it's not close. It's far.

The whole saga serves to weaken the premise that the Mavs' frontcourt was a definite strength coming into the season. The forwards, led by Flagg and a mildly resurgent PJ Washington, are playing well, but the center position remains a revolving door of things thrown against a wall. The ancient Lopez may find himself in a comfortable spot against the Mavs on Saturday as Dallas looks for a stopgap, but the rest of the team can compensate in other matchups.

The Mavericks have done well against 3-point shooters to start the season, with opponents shooting just 33.4% from distance against them through 15 games. That's the fifth-best mark in the league, and before you make the "variance" claim, just know, that includes a game at the Celtics in which Boston made 23-of-55 (42%) and at the Timberwolves in which Minnesota canned 15-of-32 (47%).

There appears to be something to the Mavs' ability to close out on shooters. If Dallas can limit the Clippers' production from deep, the Mavericks have the ability to make quick work of a Los Angeles side that seems to lack identity above all else.

The Mavericks and the Clippers tip off from American Airlines Center shortly after 7 p.m. on Saturday. Catch the game locally on KFAA Channel 29 or on a sister station throughout the team's regional viewership area. The stream will be on MavsTV or NBA League pass where available. The radio broadcast will be on KRLD 105.3 The Fan.

#2857. Colts officially place Daniel Jones on waivers

Daniel Jones is officially on the waiver wire.

The NFL transaction report has gone out this afternoon, and Jones appears as a waiver from the Colts. After the trade deadline, every player who gets released by his team has to go on waivers before he becomes a free agent.

That means any team that wants Jones on his current contract can claim him, but that is extraordinarily unlikely to happen. As the waiver transaction notes, Jones is a vested veteran with a fully guaranteed salary for the rest of this season. No team is going to want to claim that salary.

Once he clears waivers, however, he’ll be an unrestricted free agent, free to sign with any team, and it won’t be surprising if some team wants to add him for veteran depth. Jones has been terrible this season, but he’s been a competent starter at times in his career, and a team that wants an experienced backup may be interested in signing him for a minimal salary.

Jones was also waived almost exactly two years ago, on November 22, 2024. He cleared waivers and signed with the Vikings’ practice squad before signing with the Colts after that season.

#2855. Raiders Week 11: Fernando Mendoza’s arm, legs key against stingy Broncos

With just 186 total points allowed in nine games, the Denver Broncos boast the ninth-best defensive unit in that regard, which will make it tough sledding for the Las Vegas Raiders this Sunday.

The Silver & Black Express rolls into Mile High with a new conductor in prized rookie quarterback Fernando Mendoza a road divisional matchup against the first-place Broncos (tied with the Kansas City Chiefs at 7-2 overall). The No. 1 overall pick in the 2026 NFL Draft, Mendoza made his first career start against the stifling Seattle Seahawks defense and Las Vegas fell 21-13 in Week 10.

Another formidable defense awaits Mendoza in Week 11 as Vance Joseph's Denver squad comes off a bye week after a wire-to-wire Week 9 26-21 win at the Carolina Panthers two Sunday's ago.

To hear the 23-year-old budding franchise quarterback tell it, however, he wouldn't have it any other way as he preps for his second-career NFL start this Sunday in Denver.

"I believe getting thrown in the fire is the best way to learn, and the Seahawks and the Broncos both have considerably elite defenses," Mendoza said when asked what it's like getting thrown into the fire against two quality NFL defenses. "I really believe every NFL defense is elite because when you're playing at this level, the margin for error is so small, and all the defensive coordinators are geniuses.

"So, I'm honored and blessed to go against a great defensive coordinator in Coach Robbie (Raiders defensive coordinator Rob Leonard) every single day, especially in OTAs and fall camp and training camp. So, I believe that's helped prepare me, an now I'm excited to embark on this journey with my teammates."

How Mendoza settles during his second start against an aggressive Broncos defense bears watching. The Seahawks produced a blueprint of how to and how not to defend the Indiana product last week. When protected with a clean (and sometimes noisy) pocket, Mendoza was a smooth operator going through progressions and using his accurate and strong arm to deliver the football to Raiders pass catchers.

In the first half, Mendoza went 11-of-13 for 90 yards including his first-career touchdown run. When Seattle saw enough of that, head coach and defensive play caller Mike Macdonald went to his bag of tricks of blitz, simulated pressure, and exotic alignments to take advantage of the neophyte signal caller and Mendoza went 6-of-16 for 53 yards in the second half while absorbing six sacks and 12 hits.

Expect Joseph to stick to a similar script and bring the pain on the 6-foot-5 and 225-pound Raiders quarterback. Especially considering the Broncos have 84 quarterback hits on the season alongside 26 sacks. Zach Allen is an ultra-productive defensive lineman who plays both inside as a tackle and outside as an edge rusher and paces Denver with 4.5 sacks and 23 quarterback hits. Edge rusher Nik Bonitto is right there with him with 4.5 sacks and 11 QB hits while defensive tackle Malcolm Roach is disruptive with four sacks and 13 quarterback hits.

Heat-seeking inside linebacker Alex Singleton is a quality clean-up man and is tied for the league lead in tackles at 107 (Raiders inside linebacker Nakobe Dean is who he is tied with) and also has two sacks and six quarterback hits on his stat sheet.

All of this is presented to note Mendoza's accurate arm and fresh legs are integral to Las Vegas' aspirations to walk out of Denver with an AFC West win. That dual-threat ability is essential to counter the Joseph's defense that is every bit as creative and chaotic as Macdonald's group. All it takes is a few successful scrambles to make a defense bite — Seattle did this — and with Mendoza lauded for his cerebral ways and ability to not make the same mistake again, watching the film from last Sunday's loss will showcase just that.

The second-quarter 22-yard dart Mendoza zipped to wide receiver Cody White is an example of this. Mendoza felt pressure, began to scramble but kept his eyes downfield, and his ability to thrown on the arm and velocity on the fast ball were on full display.

"We have a great strength and conditioning staff here, and performance staff as a whole, whether it's the training staff, nutrition, strength. There's so many different aspects of it, so I'm blessed to be a part of it," Mendoza began when as he answered a question about his mobility being an underrated aspect of his game, "I believe I've gotten faster since being here, and I'm grateful for that because in the NFL, those players are fast."

Those fleet feet will come in handy to escape the exotic blitzes Joseph is expected to deploy. Especially with Las Vegas ground game grinding to a halt consistently over the course of the 2026 season, much to the chagrin of Raiders rookie head coach and offensive play caller Klint Kubiak. Mendoza's ability to run helps provide an alternative run game and scrambling for hidden yards and first downs can frustrate the opposing defense.

Kubiak should again go play-action heavy with designed rollouts and bootlegs to vary the launch point of the ball and give his talented rookie cleaner passing lane and run-pass options. Mendoza has the arm talent to push the ball downfield and by getting the ball into the hands of his pass catchers quickly or downfield, Las Vegas can take advantage of Denver's penchant to whiff on tackles as the team has a high 16.2 percent missed tackle rate. Of course, it'd help to have a healthy Brock Bowers available as the tight end is the Raiders' de facto WR1.

Mendoza is keen on balancing staying in the pocket and winning with this arm while also knowing when to run and gain positive yards against Denver.

"There were a lot of moments in the game that we reviewed after in the film, whether it's Coach Kubiak, Coach (Andrew) Janocko or Coach (Mike) Sullivan, they made comments on whatever the situation was, whether it was some inefficiency or a play that in the future we want to change. So, I'm taking those day by day and trying to improve on them," Mendoza said regarding staying in the pocket longer to deliver a throw instead of taking off as quickly as he did in his first start.

While the Broncos boast the ninth-best red zone defense — teams have visited the space between the 20-yard line and end zone 29 times for only 15 touchdowns for a 51.7 percent conversion rate — they are susceptible to getting yards piled upon them allowing 3,195 yards for 27th most amongst the 32 teams.

That right there is a textbook example of a bend-but-not-break defense.

Sunday's Mile High tilt marks the 134th time the Raiders and Broncos go toe-to-toe. The Silver & Black lead the all-time series 73-58-2 (72-5702 in regular season play, 1-1 in the playoffs) but are riding a four-game losing skid against the Horsemen. The last time Las Vegas beat Denver was in January of 2024, a 27-14 victory inside Allegiant Stadium. Since that tilt, the Broncos have won the next four meetings 34-18, 29-19, 10-7, and 24-17. The pair of longtime AFC West foes will meet again in Week 15 in Las Vegas.

"We were in a zone, it was an extended play, and expecting a quick throw and it became something — when extended plays happen, we always talk about the second play, and plaster, and those types of things. Anytime something doesn't go well I wish I would have called something different."

—Las Vegas Raiders defensive coordinator Rob Leonard on Seattle Seahawks wideout Jaxon Smith-Njigba's 71-yard touchdown on a broken play

"I think with Brock (Bowers) and anyone in the tight end, wide receiver, anyone, they're constantly talking and as they grow and mesh together it's something that will continue to benefit us as an offense."

—Las Vegas Raiders offensive coordinator Andrew Janocko on the chemistry between pass catchers and quarterback Fernando Mendoza

#2848. Choctaw football routs Columbia, advances to 5th straight region final

LAKE CITY — Frank Beasley has had his share of big wins at Choctawhatchee.

An 11-4 postseason record over five straight region finals appearances paints that picture, yet Friday's 32-7 Region 1-4A semifinal at Columbia felt like a bigger statement.

On a night when the rain turned sideways and "The Jungle's" natural grass field became a mudbath, Carter Marracco scored three touchdowns, Dante Whidbee rushed for 130 yards. Choctaw's defense forced two turnovers and had a pair of safeties against a one-dimensional Tigers' offense that couldn't run the ball and didn't complete a single pass on the night.

"We wanted it more than them," Marracco said. "They did all that talking on the internet and we let our play do all the talking tonight. It fired us up and our offensive line opened gaps. We were running with feet deep in the mud and getting it done."

State champ: Fort Walton Beach swimmer Brenden Hamel wins 200-yard freestyle gold

Here was a Tigers team ranked second to only American Heritage, a juggernaut sporting an 11-0 record with a 400-125 margin of victory. Here was a vaunted defense with three straight shutouts and four overall, the Tigers not allowing a point since Oct. 16, and a quarterback with a 32/3 TD:INT ratio. Here was a program looking for redemption after last year's 27-20 playoff exit at the hands of the Big Green.

None of that mattered to Choctaw, who didn't see the conditions as an excuse but an opportunity.

"We just kept grinding on them and then our defense did a good job of adjusting after the first series to their run game, the things they were trying to do," Choctaw coach Frank Beasley said. "Our defense is really hard to deal with. I think we can force teams to become one dimensional, and that's what happened tonight. Obviously with this field and the wet balls and everything that went on, it's easy to lose focus. We didn't. This is our 15th playoff game in five years. Our kids know how to prepare for these moments. They fully understand what deep playoff runs look like, what going on the road looks like."

Choctaw, whose team bus blew a tire on the four-hour drive, came out playing with its hair on fire.

Following an Amir Reese 60-yards kickoff return to open the night, it was Whidbee who set the tone early on third-and-23. The senior bursts through the line, broke a tackle 10 yards short of the first-down marker and carried another tackler to the sticks. Three plays later, Marracco punched it in from a yard out.

"It was big. You gotta do what you gotta do to get your team rolling," Whidbee said. "Sunshine or rain, we're going to do our thing and get downfield."

Later in the second quarter, in the midst of Choctaw's defense allowing 10 first-half yards to Columbia's offense, Baker Giles took a third-and-goal snap and fired across his body to Marracco, who strolled into the end zone for the 10-yard score and a 14-0 halftime lead for the Big Green.

Come the second half, Choctaw started the scoring with a safety, got Marracco's third score on a 3-yard run, added a second safety and then celebrated Giles' second touchdown on a 2-yard keeper.

Up 32-0, it was only when Choctaw lifted its starters that Columbia was able to get out of the red in yardage and score on a 73-yard run. Isaiah Matthews and TJ Franklin were

Now the Big Green will hit the road again, this time to second-seeded St. Augustine for a rematch of last year's region final Choctaw won 31-28.

"We're excited about it and proud of our community," Beasley said. "I think we might have more fans here tonight than they did. Choctaw is a special place."

#2843. Cowboys changing things up in search of consistency vs. Titans

Both the Dallas Cowboys, and their closest division rival this season, the New York Giants, host opponents from the AFC South this weekend. If the Cowboys were even living up to half of the expectations of this season, this Week 11 schedule would look like a golden opportunity to separate from the Giants, as they face the 7-2 Jacksonville Jaguars while the Cowboys have the 1-8 Tennessee Titans.

Instead, the Cowboys already blew an opportunity to win in the same week the Giants lost against the San Francisco 49ers. Even if the Giants do lose as home underdogs to the Jaguars, the Cowboys have invited enough added drama this week with a change of defensive play-callers to make the Titans feel like another handful of an opponent.

This is especially true considering how close both other games against AFC South foes have been this year, both in the win column, but neither convincing. Getting a convincing win is something Cowboys fans simply do not have faith in their team doing at all right now, and not something the Titans have given away easily despite struggling under the new coaching of Robert Saleh and offensive coordinator Brian Daboll.

Hope for the playoffs will still feel thin even with a win Dallas is expected to get against these Titans. The Cowboys have made their bed with this roster and coaching staff and will have to withstand however many embarrassing losses can come from it, with the Titans having the potential to be the worst of 2026.

It is time for our weekly look at how the Cowboys avoid this loss and go into Thanksgiving with a chance at a winning streak again, or what could lead to the latest AT&T Stadium embarrassment after all.

Every other week for the Cowboys passing attack seems to feel like a CeeDee Lamb feature game right now, with Lamb having a big say in the win over the Colts in week nine, but it was George Pickens' best game of the season with three touchdowns in week ten agaisnt the 49ers.

The fact we are still waiting into late November for a game where both Lamb and Pickens are established in a game plan and get going at the same time is a horrible sign for play-caller Brian Schottenheimer. Beating bad AFC teams is one thing he's proven capable of though, and if now isn't the time to see both Lamb and Pickens unleashed downfield, it may never come.

The Titans defense is effectively tied with the Las Vegas Raiders for worst in the league in yards per passing attempt allowed. All three of their cornerbacks that led the team in snaps last week against the Jaguars are new to the team this season – Cor'Dale Flott, Alontae Taylor, and Joshua Williams.

Tennessee is also the fourth worst team at getting to the quarterback this season with 14 sacks, just one more than the 49ers team the Cowboys offense scored 40 points against last week. The Cowboys defense is more of an unknown than ever before coming into this game after already underperforming all season, so the adage that Dallas can win this game by having Dak Prescott simply outplay Cam Ward by a big margin is not a reliable one.

That said, another huge Prescott game against this opponent where he's able to effectively get the ball deep to both of his top receivers should be enough to get in the win column. The Cowboys have beaten Robert Saleh defenses consistently before, and will have to do so again to keep faint NFC East hopes alive this Sunday.

The football gods always seem to work in mysterious ways, in the sense they will test the very thing a team is struggling with in the blink of an eye. The Cowboys reasoning for having the veteran coach Mike Pettine take over defensive play-calling from rookie Christian Parker has more layers than an onion. One of the more sound reasons though is that the team is heavily invested financially in their front seven, where Quinnen Williams has looked more and more like a cornerstone player to build around each week in his first full season with the team.

Pettine will likely look to run the defense with a front-centric mindset, focusing on this front seven first and then the backend, where as Parker was the opposite. For a coach that wasn't here for any offseason activity at all, and only been in the building since October, his first test of this new approach comes against a challenging opponent.

With last year's first overall pick Cam Ward at quarterback, the Titans rank in the 98th percentile in their utilization of the quick passing game this season. They have yet another dual-threat, home run hitting running back in former Cowboy Tony Pollard. Ward's best trait has always been his ability to throw the ball out of a moving pocket, and the Titans are figuring out in real time how to protect him and also target the middle of the field at a high rate to open up the sophomore passer's game.

The Titans lone win this season came in week six against the Colts, but this is what Ward's passing chart looked like:

For comparison, the Titans didn't beat the Jaguars last week, but they put up numbers offensively and played well, and this is how Ward threw the ball to do so:

If Mike Pettine is going to make a difference for the Cowboys after they just allowed a franchise record for yards given up in a home game, to a 49ers team that exposed them in the middle of the field, with a mobile QB as well, doing so against the Titans would actually be a solid start.

With the ball coming out so quickly from Ward's hands, the Cowboys likely can't rely on the pass rush element they got against the 49ers with five sacks either. Pass rush will have to come from sound coverage in the short to intermediate part of the field, where the Titans will look to stress Shemar James, Caleb Downs, and even Rashan Gary who got up field without contain on a number of big 49ers plays last week.

If the Cowboys cannot handle these things, and also struggle to get multiple bodies to Pollard in the run game and allow him to get around the edge or push the pile, it could be yet another long afternoon for Brian Schottenheimer's team against an inferior opponent.

#2840. Game Preview #17: Wolves vs Grizzlies Thread

Remember when there was a "budding rivalry" between the Minnesota Timberwolves and Memphis Grizzlies? A drama filled first round playoff matchup between future faces of the league, Anthony Edwards and Ja Morant. Karl-Anthony Towns Sr. and Tee Morant became the cutest "Dads of the NBA" bromance. Dillon Brooks, Mike Conley, Kyle Anderson…just so many storylines.

Now these two teams couldn't appear to be any further away from that. The Grizzlies have zero players remaining on their 2022 roster. The Wolves roster just Anthony Edwards and Jaden McDaniels from their respective squad. The connection these two franchises share is more about who gets to join the Eastern Conference in a few years. In the meantime, contrary to what many thought, Memphis is standing right above Minnesota in the Western Conference standings!

Who: Minnesota Timberwolves (11-5) vs. Memphis Grizzlies (12-4)

When: Saturday, November 21 at 7:00 PM CT

If you haven't been paying attention, the Memphis Grizzlies have been one of the feel-good stories of the NBA so far. They completely, and rightfully, ditched their previous oft-injured core of Ja Morant, Jaren Jackson Jr., and Desmond Bane. Many pundits believed that Memphis would be among the worst teams in the league as they rebuild from the ground floor this year (not me, as I was wise beyond comprehension).

Instead, they were shot out of a cannon to begin the season, starting 6-0. No, I'm not talking about a 6-0 scoring run. I'm talking about a 6-0 team record. They follow Tommy Ice/T.I.'s (head coach Tuomas Iisalo) lead, employing a strategy where he spreads minutes across a 10 to 12-man rotation. 11 different players average over 15 minutes per game. They're led by early season MVP candidate Ty Jerome (missed previous four games) and 2026 third overall draft pick, Cameron Boozer.

They fell back to Earth by losing four of their next five games, but have already bounced back to win five straight (against admittedly relatively easy competition). When you zoom out further, the only "good teams" they've beaten are the Trail Blazers and Cavaliers. They've had the easiest strength of schedule by a considerable margin.

So are they for real? More real than most thought.

Equal opportunity to all. That might be on the Grizzlies' whiteboard before every game. 11 different players average over 5.0 field goal attempts per game, compared to just eight for Minnesota. Nine different players average 9.0+ points per game, compared to just seven for Minnesota.

You cannot just guard one player to stop Memphis.

For Minnesota, this means their league-best defense will need to be on a string. Everyone is dangerous and they have to be treated as such. As a team, the Grizzlies make the third most amount of triples per game (15.6) and drain them at a league-best rate (39.6%) – most of these being open attempts. They are third in assists per game. They will spread things around and make themselves unpredictable.

Don't be surprised if a Cam Spencer or Javon Small pop off for 16 points. It's very possible Jerami Grant has a MPE (medium player explosion) and suddenly scores 24 points out of nowhere. Where did Taylor Hendricks come from, and why is he so buff and athletic now? Memphis also has no shortage of quality role players like Cedric Coward and Jaylen Wells, who will muck the game up on both ends of the court.

What has happened to LaMelo Ball's offense? (Did you see what I did? I removed the "O" from his name above. Like he's playing so bad offensively he doesn't have "O" in his name. Do you get it?) After I spent some time developing (more) creative headlines so that I could gush about his defense, it's been quite jarring to see his drop-off in what's supposed to be his main talent.

This month of November has been a seesaw of production so far. If you compare the numbers in the first five games of the month when the Wolves went 5-0, to his most recent four game stretch, it's eye-popping.

If you've been watching the games, you'll see that Ball is clearly trying to shoot his way through this slump. In fact, he's taking three more shots per game during this most recent stretch. Is this something more than just a rough patch? LaMelo had some tape around his left hand which he tugged at during throughout Thursday's game. There hasn't been any reports of ailments, at least not to my knowledge.

We're not at Rudy Gobert levels of offensive concern yet, but I'm keeping me eye on this.

When's the last time the Wolves had a blowout win? Over a week? Yeah, that's too long. It's time for Minnesota to bump up that point differential and pad some stats. LaMelo Ball finally breaks out of his slump and busts up the Grizzlies for a cool 33 points thanks to eight three-pointers. Rudy Gobert gives Cam Boozer his "Welcome the NBA" moment by dunking all over his head off an alley-oop.

#2838. Nicolas Cage on his John Madden misfire: "I'm not Frank Caliendo"

This whole thing is antithetical to my identity.

That’s the one line that resonates most strongly in the new Madden movie. Because it’s exactly what John Madden would say if he saw it.

It’s bad. It’s very bad. Anyone who says it’s good has no taste and/or an agenda that involves the strategic application of lips to asses.

The biggest problem is Nicolas Cage as John Madden. Anyone who vividly remembers the unique charisma of John Madden knows instantly that nothing about Cage’s portrayal reflects the true nature, spirit, and demeanor of one of the most important and impactful figures in the entire history of the sport of football.

Cage, who seems to realize that his portrayal missed not only the bull’s-eye but also the entire wall on which the dart board was mounted, has defended the outcome by peering down his nose at the sports world.

“He was not on my radar,” Cage said of Madden, via Kirsten Chuba of the Hollywood Reporter. “I was growing up watching black-and-white Orson Welles movies and Fellini movies and Bruce Lee. I wasn’t interested in team sports whatsoever.”

“I’m someone that likes to stay out of his comfort zone and take a risk, I said yes, because that would be a transformation,” Cage explained regarding the decision to attempt to play the towering, iconic figure. “It would be something where I might learn a few things, taking on this character who is really nothing like me.”

The audience has learned that Cage’s interpretation is nothing like Madden.

“That’s what acting’s about,” Cage said. “I’m not an impressionist, I’m not Frank Caliendo. I lean more towards being a surrealist, meaning I like to get a concept of a personality and then dig deep and see what I can locate emotionally with my imagination and my psyche and filter it through the cadence and the appearance, within reason, of John Madden. I’m trying to give you an evocation, more or less, or an interpretation.”

The problem was it wasn’t even “within reason.” It was without reason. And the entire film was beyond unreasonable.

And I like Nicolas Cage. He was brilliant in Moonstruck. He won an Oscar for Leaving Las Vegas. And The Family Man remains a highly underrated holiday film.

Still, to quote another holiday film, “They can’t all be winners.” Cage’s role in Madden as John Madden is not.

I only met John Madden once. I watched him on TV for decades. I’ve heard plenty of stories about him from people who worked with him at NBC. And I’m confident that he would be livid about this film.

“That’s not me,” he’d say. “Not even close.”

Or, perhaps, “This whole thing is antithetical to my identity.”

#2836. Daniel Jones has been released by the Colts. Should the Patriots go after him?

You heard about a Friday news dump, but have you heard about a Friday QB dump? That's precisely what the Indianapolis Colts did when they released their starting quarterback, Daniel Jones, earlier today.

Jones, 29, was in his second season with the franchise and just eight months removed from signing a two-year, $88 million contract extension. However, he and the Colts as a whole have struggled mightily this year, leading to some sweeping changes within the organization. First, they fired the GM who signed him to that deal, Chris Ballard, and now they let go of Jones himself.

His release comes with the Colts at 3-8 and Jones having completed 205 of 351 pass attempts (58.4%) for 1,974 yards with seven touchdowns and 13 interceptions. A year removed from tearing his Achilles tendon, he is ranked 30th among 31 qualifying quarterbacks in EPA per play this season (-0.139) and 31st in both net yards per attempt (4.8) and completion percentage above expectation (-6.1).

Long story short, he has been dreadful. And yet, the New England Patriots might just keep a close eye on him.

The Patriots, after all, saw their own starting QB, Drake Maye, suffer a fractured fibula during last Sunday's loss versus the Detroit Lions. While it remains to be seen how long he will be out, Maye missing most if not all of his team's seven final regular season games this year would not come as a surprise.

The question now becomes whether or not the Patriots should pursue Jones as a replacement. Let's make the argument in favor of and against such a move.

Before making this argument, it needs to be prefaced with this: everything in this section only applies to Jones going through the waiver wire unclaimed and making it to the open market. According to Jason Fitzgerald of Over the Cap, a team would need $6.35 million in salary cap space to claim Jones — financial flexibility the Patriots do not have at the moment. If he passes waivers, he could be signed to a deal worth as little as $505,554.

With that out of the way, let's take a look at why brining Jones in would make sense for the Patriots. It all starts with one question: would he be an upgrade over Tommy DeVito for however long Drake Maye will be sidelined?

His play this season so far suggests that that might not be the case. However, a change in environment might help Jones regain some of the confidence he had in his first year with the Colts, when he played some of the best football of his career and finished the season sixth in the league in EPA per play (0.201). If the Patriots can get a fraction of that, they should be happy with Jones as a better alternative than DeVito.

In addition, Jones also has some familiarity with the Patriots scheme. While he never played directly under offensive coordinator Josh McDaniels, he did spend the better part of three seasons under former New England assistant Brian Daboll. The offense Daboll ran with the Giants has similarities to McDaniels' in terms of terminology, quarterback responsibility and play designs, meaning that the lead-up time for Jones to get ready would be comparatively small.

Inserting Jones would likely also not come with significant philosophical changes. In essence, after all, he is a similar player type as Maye is: somebody capable of making plays with his arm as well as his legs, as evidenced by his career rushing average of 6.1 yards per carry.

While his highs have not been close to Maye's, he has shown that he can be an adequate NFL starting quarterback and somebody able to elevate the talent around him. That obviously was not the case in Indianapolis this season, but if a change of scenery and move to a better-run team can get him close to that, it would be precisely what the Patriots need right now if there are any doubts about DeVito.

And even if not, what do the Patriots have to lose? They are 5-5 and fighting for their playoff lives, and signing Jones would not automatically disqualify them from starting DeVito. At the very least, he would provide veteran depth better suited to challenge the post-Maye hierarchy than sixth-round rookie Behren Morton.

On Friday, Jones became the first quarterback in the NFL in more than three decades to get released less than 24 hours after starting a game. That plus the fact the Colts are willing to cut their losses despite the financial investment they made in him just this offseason should tell you all you need to know.

While there might be a snowballing effect to Jones' struggles and a fresh start could indeed help him regain the form he previously showed, there is no denying he has played some of the worst football in the NFL this year. The numbers mentioned above are an illustration of that.

Some of it had to do with the surroundings and a Colts team that a) lacking quality across the board, and b) is in a free fall since October. In addition, his injury situation also seems to have played a part in it: 12 months after his torn Achilles, he still does not look like he is close to 100% and fails to consistently to step into throws, maneuver the pocket and pick up yards on the ground, averaging just 3.8 yards per carry.

On top of it, Jones has been a streaky player throughout his career. While there have been stretches of play where he lived up to his former first-round status, the lows have been underwhelming, including this season.

Given all of that, expecting a quick turnaround in New England would be naive, particularly considering that the Patriots are missing several offensive pieces because of injury. But even if they didn't, Jones would not have any familiarity with them: despite some experience in a McDaniels-style system, he would have to build chemistry with his receiving corps, offensive line and coaching staff on the fly.

Tommy DeVito, meanwhile, has a two-year head start in that regard. Speaking of DeVito, he was signed to a contract extension in the offseason to serve as QB2 and has, by all accounts, been a model citizen deserving of his shot. Obviously, the job should go to the best option regardless of contract situation. That said, all things considered — including what a change might mean for the locker room — DeVito still has a strong case to be just that.

Even though they are still in a competitive position on paper, the Patriots are fighting for their playoff lives with Drake Maye sidelined for the foreseeable future. Their margin for error, meanwhile, is, to quote safety Kevin Byard, "basically at zero."

Would adding Jones increase it and allow New England to play winning football during Maye's absence? Ideally, that is not a question they would have to ask; Tommy DeVito stepping in and leading the offense at an adequate level would be the best-case scenario. Of course, as the Lions game showed, there is a realistic chance the lights are too bright for him.

The problem is, they also might be for Jones. So far this season, after all, he has not been good and not been able to hold up to the pressure of being the guy at quarterback in Indianapolis. He wouldn't quite be that in New England, sure, but the eyes of one of the NFL's biggest markets would still be on him.

Would that prevent the Patriots from signing him? Probably not. Still, it is one of the factors to consider alongside the arguments outlined above. And putting all of those together, it is clear the decision whether or not to sign Jones as a potential stand-in for Maye is quite nuanced. At the end of the day, Mike Vrabel, Eliot Wolf and Ryan Cowden — the latter of whom spent time with Jones in New York — will have to see their way through it.

And once they do, they very well might find that the potential disruption caused by adding Jones might not be worth the theoretical gains.

What do you think about Daniel Jones? Should the Patriots pursue him, or steer clear of him? Please head down to the comment section to share your thoughts, or jump into the following discussion thread set up by community member Gonzucla in the Feed.

#2833. Las Vegas Raiders-Denver Broncos FEED Topic: Kolton Miller, DJ Glaze vs. Nik Bonitto, Jonathon Cooper

The Seattle Seahawks managed to get six sacks on Fernando Mendoza in his starting debut last week, and pressure on the quarterback will be another major key to the game for the Las Vegas Raiders' offense in Week 11. The Denver Broncos present the same problem but in a different way than Seattle, one that will place a greater emphasis on the Raiders' offensive line, especially tackles Kolton Miller and DJ Glaze.

Led by head coach Mike Macdonald, the Seahawks defense uses a creative scheme to help manufacture pressure and create confusion for opposing quarterbacks. To a certain extent, they're able to work around or neutralize the offensive line by attacking running backs in pass protection or forcing quarterbacks to account for free rushers by throwing hot. Last week was a perfect example, as top cornerback Devon Witherspoon led the team with two of the six sacks.

Meanwhile, Broncos defensive coordinator Vance Joseph isn't afraid to blitz, but that's to help create one-on-one opportunities for their defensive linemen, especially edge rushers Nik Bonitto and Jonathon Cooper.

Bonitto is coming off back-to-back Pro Bowl seasons, where he registered 27.5 sacks and 133 pressures, per Pro Football Focus, in 2024 and 2025 combined. This season, he's recorded 4.5 sacks in nine games and ranks tied for 19th among edges with 30 pressures.

Meanwhile, Cooper notched 18.5 sacks and 112 pressures in the two previous seasons to give the Broncos a quality No. 2 pass-rusher on the edge. In 2026, he's only participated in three games due to off-field issues in the offseason and has just 10 pressures and 0.5 sacks on 77 pass-rush snaps. So, Cooper's numbers are down as a consequence of his actions, but his track record on the field speaks for itself.

On the other side of the trenches, Miller has an impressive resume in pass protection but has been regressing this year. With 32 pressures on 379 opportunities, per PFF, his 8.4 percent pressure rate is the highest it's been since the 2018 first-round pick's rookie year (11.0 percent). But Glaze is trending in the opposite direction, going from 8.3 percent and 8.0 percent pressure rates in his first two seasons, respectively, to 4.0 percent (15 pressures on 379 opportunities) this year.

With the Raiders shifting from Kirk Cousins to Mendoza last week and seeing their playoff hopes rapidly decline over the last roughly two months, we've reached the point in the season where it's time to start looking at building for the future. Part of that is making sure the rookie quarterback can stay upright enough to make some plays and build confidence heading into next year. The other part is evaluating the team's needs before the offseason gets going.

It would be remiss to talk about the Broncos' pass rush and not mention Zach Allen, who currently leads all defensive tackles with 38 pressures and has 4.5 sacks. However, it feels like a foregone conclusion that Las Vegas will have to address its guard situation this spring. Meanwhile, offensive tackle is still up in the air, and this weekend will be a good litmus test for Miller and Glaze.

Is the veteran left tackle past his prime, where it's time to find a new blind side protector? Is the third-year pro's growth legitimate? How they fare against one of the NFL's best edge-rush duos over the last few years, Bonitto and Cooper, on Sunday will go a long way toward answering those questions.

So, for this week's game day discussion on The FEED, we'll be keeping tabs on how well Miller and Glaze can protect the edges to keep Mendoza clean in Denver. The game kicks off at 1:25 p.m. PST and will air on CBS (check your local listings). I look forward to talkin' some ball with y'all!

#2829. Cy Young winners Jacob Misiorowski and Cam Schlittler just put up a 2026 tandem not seen since 1905

If not listed, please contact your TV provider.

To no one's real surprise, Jacob Misiorowski of the Milwaukee Brewers and Cam Schlittler of the New York Yankees have been named the 2026 Cy Young Award winners for, respectively, the National League and American League. Each was announced as the winner of Major League Baseball's topmost honor for pitchers, and each won the balloting in unanimous fashion. They are the first duo to win unanimously in the same year in their respective leagues since Justin Verlander and Sandy Alcantara in 2022 and just the third in MLB history.

Obviously, Cy Young winners have necessarily authored excellent seasons, but in the case of this year's winners, the descriptor "excellent" doesn't quite rise to meet the work of Misiorowski and Schlittler:

We could get into stuff, yes, as each has a blazing fastball that overwhelms hitters at the front end of a deep repertoire. This exercise, though, is more about how Misiorowski and Schlittler have overwhelmed the opposition when it comes to keeping runs off the board. You'll note all the strikeouts above, of course, but what may catch the eye is that each boasts a sub-2.00 ERA. That's a time-honored signifier of pitcher impeccability, even if ERA has its flaws.

To upgrade this familiar stat just a bit – and to put the two 2026 Cy Young winners' accomplishments into fitting context – we'll turn to ERA+. ERA+ is ERA adjusted to reflect league and home ballpark tendencies. An ERA+ of 100 represents the league average, and each point above or below that mark reflects a one-point change. For example, a pitcher with an ERA+ of 120 had a park- and league-adjusted ERA that was 20% better than the league average, while a pitcher with an ERA+ of 95 was 5% worse than the league average. So from the hurlers' standpoint, the higher the ERA+, the better.

This is all a necessary step when comparing pitchers across eras, because run–scoring environments have changed so drastically over the history of the sport. For example, Pedro Martinez's ERA of 1.74 in 2000, when teams were averaging more than five runs per game, means a lot more than, say, Charlie Chech's 1.74 ERA in 1908, when teams averaged 3.38 runs per game. That's where ERA+ comes in. Martinez in 2000 had an ERA+ of 291, or a park-adjusted ERA that was 191% (!) better than the league average. Chech in 1908 had the same ERA as Martinez in 2000, but his ERA+ of 136 was drastically worse.

As for the two moundsmen of present interest, Misiorowski this past season had an ERA+ of 232, which led the majors. Schlittler had an ERA+ of 215, which led the American League. Yes, they had those sub-2.00 ERAs, but, really, topping a 200 ERA+ is a much more impressive achievement. Counting Misiorowski and Schlittler but throwing out the pitchers who achieved the mark in the 60-game 2020 season (you should always throw out 2020), just 43 pitchers in MLB since 1900 have managed a 200 ERA+ or better across a qualifying number of innings. Over that same span and with 2020 also omitted, 201 pitchers have achieved a sub-2.00 ERA in a qualifying number of innings.

As you can imagine, it's a rarity to have not one but two pitchers in the same season who have a 200 ERA+ or better, as was the case this past season with Misiorowski and Schlittler. How rare? No season has ever given us more than two such qualifiers. Here's the full list of those across all of modern MLB history (i.e., since 1900):

Jacob Misiorowski, Brewers (232); Cam Schlittler, Yankees (215)

Jacob deGrom, Mets (218); Blake Snell, Rays (217)

Jake Arrieta, Cubs (215), Zack Greinke, Dodgers (222)

Roger Clemens, Blue Jays (222); Pedro Martinez, Expos (219)

Carl Lundgren, Cubs (211); Jack Pfiester, Cubs (214)

Christy Mathewson, Giants (233); Ed Reulbach, Cubs (209)

Yes, just six seasons have given us more than one 200 ERA+ pitcher, and this year's Miz-Cam combo marks just the third time that we've had one from each league. Accordingly, this marks just the third time that each Cy Young winner has reached the 200 ERA+ benchmark in a given season.

There's another, even more exclusive group that puts the Miz-Cam tandem in the proper context and hints at a future that's as bright as their present and their recent past. In 2026, Schlittler was in his age-25 campaign, while Misiorowski was in his age-24 season. So they're young as frontline aces go, and while you never want to make assumptions about pitcher health and durability in the current era, this duo's elite performance at both top-line and underlying level raises the possibility that each will win another Cy or two (or three) in the years to come.

Turning back to the 200 ERA+ defining stat, such youth in tandem with such excellence is all but unprecedented. Of the seasons listed just above, just 1905 featured two pitchers who, like Miz and Cam this past season, were age 25 or younger at the time. Mathewson, the inner-circle all-time great, was 24 that season and Reulbach was a 22-year-old rookie. We haven't seen something like that in more than 120 years.

Let's also note that Misiorowski and Shlittler, who each struck out more than 30% of opposing batters this season, absolutely have the stuff and foundational, forward-looking results to continue lording over their guild. Until that comes to pass, though, let's appreciate ther historical rare success and their even more historically rare combination of success and youth.

This season, he was named the starter for Week 1. Jones started all 11 games for a listless offensive unit which has been selling off their key players. Their offensive line is absolutely porous. This year, Jones hasn't had any time to throw and has paltry production such as 144 (twice) yards, 108 (twice), 143, and 166 in games. He has thrown seven touchdowns against 13 interceptions.

For his eight-year career, Jones' stats: 94 games with 93 starts, 1,903 completions on 2,976 attempts for 19,657 yards, converted 979 first downs, 96 TDs, 68 picks, a 63.9% completion ratio, sacked 257 times, an 84.1 QB rating, and a 35-57-1 starting win-loss record. He is a very good runner with 478 carries for 2,441 yards with another 21 scores.

Thursday night, the Colts lost to the then-3-6-0 Houston Texans 26-3 in a totally lopsided contest. Indy had 38 rushing yards while Jones went 12 for 27 for 144 yards, sacked twice and had a single pick with zero TDs.

The Colts' offense is ranked #30 in the league. They have allowed the eighth most sacks (28) and thrown the most interceptions with 13. Despite losing one of the best offensive guards in the league by trading away LG Quenton Nelson to the Seattle Seahawks before the trade deadline, the Indianapolis offensive line is ranked #17.

By claiming Jones, it could mean the Cardinals would have a capable quarterback for their remaining games this season who would compete with Gardner Minshew and Carson Beck in training camp next year.

Then again, what would be the cost to bring him in? None of the four quarterbacks on the roster are costing the franchise hardly anything.

#2827. Washington WBB runs Stanford out of the gym 105-50 behind 17 threes

Stanford was, on paper, the best team No. 16 Washington Huskies had faced to date. After a few slow starts in their first four games, the sizable crowd of 3,785 at Alaska Airlines Arena likely envisioned a competitive basketball game against a familiar opponent.

Tina Langley's squad had other ideas, however, jumping out on a 12-0 run that included a deep three from star guard Sayvia Sellers in front of Stanford's bench during the first quarter to help the Huskies jump out to a 23-8 lead after the first frame. Another run in the second quarter that ended 20-4 after 5:41 of game time had Washington up 41-15 and all but guaranteed a Huskies win as Stanford shot just 26.1 percent from the field in the first half.

"I think every game you see us improve in some category," Langley said after the game. "Half of our team is new, and so it takes a little bit of time to know each other and know new systems. We're doing new things on the defensive and offensive ends of the floor and so I think it just takes time to learn, and this is a very high IQ team. I appreciate the way that they study and spend time together learning. It's just taking us a little bit of time. I also think playing at home matters. You know, we went on the road and then you come home and you do really appreciate that feeling, and the way our community comes out and supports us does give us a lot of confidence."

In the third quarter, an Avery Howell three with 2:26 remaining in the period doubled Stanford up, moving the score to 72-35. By the time the final buzzer sounded, Langley's team had crossed the century mark for the first time in more than three years, made more threes (17) than Stanford did field goals (16), and scored more points on threes (51) than Stanford scored in total (50) in a 105-50 rout of their former Pac-12 compatriots.

All ten available Huskies played at least 10 minutes and scored; eight made at least one three, and nine attempted at least one triple (Tilda Trygger missed her only attempt of the night). Second-year forward Brynn McGaughy bounced back from fouling out on Tuesday against Washington State to lead Washington with a career-high 21 points while adding 6 rebounds and 3 steals. Howell made four threes en route to 20 points and pulled down 5 rebounds of her own.

Third-year guard Devin Coppinger set a new career high with 17, all in the first half, and had 4 offensive rebounds. Sellers was one assist shy of a double-double, scoring 12 points and dishing 9 dimes to go along with 3 steals of her own. First-year forward Amayah Garcia came off the bench to score 10 points as well.

"I think a lot of our offense honestly came from our defensive pressure," Howell said after the game. "I think that we worked really hard on our defense and scouting, which we always do for every opponent that we're going to face. But I think with the pressure we had and the strategy we had defensively allowed us to play in transition, which is one of our team's strengths. We have a lot of different people that can bring the ball up the court and get us into transition, and then being able to flow out of that helps us get a lot of high-quality shots. And I think it's hard to guard when you have a bunch of different people that can score. Anyone on the court was a threat tonight, and that's exactly why we have so much fun. We share the connection. I think that's something that we're seeing every game develop and grow, which is just so much fun to play with, and there's a lot of joy and just a lot of fun on the court together."

"We've definitely been trying to emphasize joy," McGaughy said. "And we bring that every single day, no matter the circumstances. So really trying to dig into that was a big thing, and I thought those (four converted] and-ones were a perfect opportunity to do so. So I wanted to jump on that. And you know, the crowd was going too, so that always helps, but really it's just so much fun to celebrate your teammates. So that's the biggest thing."

Stanford was able to increase their full game percentage from the field to 32.7 percent thanks to slightly better shooting in a second half that was primarily garbage time. Second-year guard Hailee Swain was the only Cardinal to get into double figures with 12 points on 5-of-15 from the field.

The colossal win jumped Washington ten spots in Bart Torvik's T-rank from No. 22 to No. 12 and dropped Stanford from No. 58 to No. 74. Stanford shot just 54.5 percent from the free throw line, turned the ball over 19 times on 11 Huskies steals, and were outscored 24-6 on the fast break by Washington. Langley's squad also racked up 23 assists to Stanford's 6 and did not allow the Cardinal to lead all evening.

"They've been working incredibly hard. You can see the growth game to game, each player," Langley said after the game. "They continue to show that they're putting a lot of time in and working incredibly hard to get used to the system and just excited to see them and get to celebrate them tonight. You're starting to see each player really understand their individual contributions and how they can help us be great. I think you can see each player tonight had a moment where you knew they'd been working on something that they did tonight, and I think that's the celebration you saw. And so that was really fun."

Washington returns to action on Sunday against Cal Poly at 2:00 p.m. at Alaska Airlines Arena before heading to Florida for Coconut Hoops against Oklahoma State and UMass after the Thanksgiving break.

#2825. Why a long-term mega deal for Tarik Skubal isn't actually all that risky

If not listed, please contact your TV provider.

The big prize of this free agent class is ace lefty and two-time Cy Young winner Tarik Skubal. We've seen and will continue to see predictions that he's going to land a deal of around 8-10 years and topping $400 million. The knee-jerk reaction to such prognostications in many corners will be that he won't possibly be "worth" that much and that such a deal would be a huge "risk" to the signing team.

Let's stop right there. I don't think it's all that risky. There's risk for the team on every contract, especially pitchers, of course, but in Skubal, the signing team is getting a surefire ace for several years.

Let's examine some of the reasons why such an enormous deal isn't actually that dangerous.

Since returning from surgery in 2023, Skubal has been absurdly good. Across his ages 26-29 seasons, he's made 103 starts and has gone 50-20 with a 2.47 ERA (169 ERA+), 0.92 WHIP and 757 strikeouts in 626 ⅓ innings. He's only walked 109, or 1.6 per nine innings. He rarely gets hit hard, he rarely gives up many runs, he strikes out a high number of hitters and rarely walks any. Simply, he's been good at everything.

The recent track record here says this is the best pitcher in baseball, at least in the long view (we could argue Paul Skenes was better in 2025 and Jacob Misiorowski was better in 2026, to name two examples, but if we looked at 2023-present, it isn't all that close).

There isn't a dropoff in the postseason, either. In eight playoff starts, Skubal has a 2.13 ERA, 0.79 WHIP and 70 strikeouts against nine unintentional walks in 50 ⅔ innings.

Also, the career workload isn't anything to be concerned about. Skubal heads to his age-30 season now with only 925 ⅓ innings in the regular season. Even counting the playoffs doesn't get Skubal to 1,000 innings at the big-league level. Let's go back to Skenes. He's only 24 years old and already has 495 career innings. Another ace? Justin Verlander had 1,553 ⅔ career innings through his age-29 season.

There just isn't a ton of tread on these Skubal tires.

We'll hear plenty of consternation about Skubal being 30 years old next season as it relates to signing a long-term deal. We'll also hear people say a deal is risky due to his injury history. He had Tommy John surgery in college, flexor tendon surgery in 2022 and arthroscopic surgery (a new procedure using a Nanoscope) in 2026.

The simple matter is that pretty much every pitcher nowadays has had some sort of surgery on his arm, notably the elbow, and we've seen pitchers time and again bounce back after elbow surgery to perform at a high level.

Skubal just did it. He only missed roughly six weeks after his procedure this past season. He came back to make 19 starts after the surgery and posted a 2.73 ERA with 141 strikeouts in 115 ⅓ innings.

I mentioned Verlander above. Skubal is a multi-time Cy Young winner, so it's acceptable to look at some of the best pitchers in baseball and how they aged into their late-30s in recent memory.

Again, Skubal will be in his age-30 season in 2027. If he signs an eight-year deal, it'll run through his age-37 season.

For those so worried about the long-term finances of the team owner who ends up signing Skubal, are you sure you're really worried that he won't be productive through five, six, seven or even all eight years of that deal? Given his track record, talent, pitching arsenal, relatively low workload given his age and how well some of the best pitchers have fared through their mid-30s, he actually seems like a great bet.

A decade ago, the common talking point was that long and huge deals for pitchers never age well. That isn't actually the case, for the most part. Here are some of the biggest pitcher contracts of all time and how they unfolded (or are unfolding).

Yoshinobu Yamamoto, Dodgers, 12 years, $325 million -- He came over from Japan for his age-25 season, so this one was a different animal. Still, it sure doesn't seem like it'll be a bad deal, does it?

Gerrit Cole, Yankees, 9 years, $324 million -- Through six years of the deal, Cole has missed a chunk of time due to Tommy John surgery. He's also won a Cy Young, finished top four in Cy Young voting three times, been worth 22.4 WAR and was the ace of an American League pennant winner.

Stephen Strasburg, Nationals, 7 years, $245 million -- Here's the bust. Strasburg was never healthy again after the 2019 World Series title run. Still, the Nationals wouldn't have that title without him.

Justin Verlander, Tigers, 10 years, $219.5 million -- This was the deal that ran from 2010-19. During this deal, Verlander won two Cy Youngs (one in the second year of the deal, one in the final year), a World Series, three pennants, 160 games and led the league in pitcher WAR three times.

Max Fried, Yankees, 8 years, $218 million -- It's only been two years, but Fried is 24-10 with a 2.83 ERA and finished fourth in AL Cy Young voting in his first year.

David Price, Red Sox, 7 years, $217 million -- He won a World Series in the third year of the contract. Overall, he was 53-26 with a 3.78 ERA (118 ERA+) through the course of the deal.

Corbin Burnes, Diamondbacks, 6 years, $210 million -- Burnes had his first year of the deal interrupted by Tommy John surgery. He had a 2.66 ERA through 11 starts prior to the injury. The jury's still out, but he heads to his age-32 season fully recovered now.

Zack Greinke, 6 years, $206.5 million -- Over the course of this deal, Greinke was 77-39 with a 3.55 ERA (125 ERA+) and 19.9 WAR. He had a fourth-place Cy Young finish in there.

CC Sabathia, 9 years, $202 million -- The Yankees won the World Series in Sabathia's first year there and he started the deal with three straight top-four Cy Young finishes. Overall, during this deal, he was 120-73 with 27.1 WAR and had five straight 200-plus inning seasons.

Max Scherzer, 7 years, $191.4 million -- During this contract, Scherzer won two Cy Youngs, a World Series title and 99 games with a 2.75 ERA. He posted 41.8 WAR. In the seven years on this deal, Scherzer finished in the top five in Cy Young voting six times, with 2020 being the outlier.

We can close with this. Some people might not care for such a measure, particularly the salary cap crowd who will be worried about the owners paying too much. Regardless, let's stick with the general belief that each individual WAR (1.0 WAR) is worth a bit over $8 million per season.

By this measure, Skubal was worth $48.4 million in 2024, $53.7 million in 2025 and $41.7 million last season. Putting aside how much less money he actually made than what he was doing on the field, and knowing everything laid out above, I'll ask a simple question:

Are you sure a 10-year, $400 million deal for this guy would be a "risk?"

I think we could argue that it would be a bargain.

#2824. Toto Wolff wants answers after George Russell misses Vegas pole despite “ideal” final-lap position

Mercedes left Friday night qualifying at the Las Vegas Strip Circuit with the fastest car on track, yet team principal Toto Wolff still had questions to answer. World champion Kimi Antonelli topped Q3 with a 1m33.893s, only to drop to sixth on the grid due to a five-place penalty for taking Mercedes' updated ADUO turbocharger. Instead of team-mate George Russell inheriting pole, Red Bull's Max Verstappen snatched top spot with a 1m34.151s - edging Russell's 1m34.224s by just 0.073 seconds.

The result stung after Russell had looked untouchable earlier in the evening, topping final practice with a 1m34.041s that put him 0.911s clear of Antonelli. Complicating matters in Q3, Russell had asked Mercedes for a slipstream from his already-penalised team-mate.

Instead, a messy final out-lap left Antonelli trailing the pack, allowing the Italian to pull a tow worth up to 16 km/h down the Strip straight from cars ahead—including Russell.

Asked after qualifying whether Mercedes should have orchestrated a better tow to aid Russell's fight for second in the Drivers' Championship, Wolff dismissed track positioning as the culprit.

"Actually, George for me had the perfect position. He was four seconds behind the car in front," Wolff said. "We need to find out because I think there was some kind of Turn 7, 8, 9 - whether there was a mistake or something else happened, I don't know, to be honest, but it looked like the ideal position for a pole lap because he was quickest all the time."

Even without pole, Russell lines up second alongside Verstappen on Saturday night with major championship stakes on the line. Sitting third in the standings and 17 points behind Verstappen, Russell has a prime chance to make serious ground after both Ferraris - Charles Leclerc (11th) and Lewis Hamilton (12th) - were knocked out in Q2.

Tension between the front-row rivals is already running hot. Late in FP3, Verstappen and Russell tangled wheel-to-wheel at Turn 14 after Verstappen passed the Mercedes and slowed into the corner, prompting the Dutchman to complain over team radio that Russell had "pushed me into the barrier". Following a post-session review, the FIA stewards handed Verstappen a warning for the skirmish.

Asked what he expected when the pair launch side by side toward Turn 1, Wolff didn't downplay the prospect of fireworks.

"Well, with cinema, if they try to go through the first corners together, we shall be watching," Wolff said.
